# Reinier Vinkeles: Study Notes

**Site:** Reinier Vinkeles (19 June 1741, Amsterdam - 30 January 1816, Amsterdam), Dutch engraver and draughtsman, Jan Punt's only attested pupil, who studied about ten years with him, joined the Amsterdam Stadstekenacademie in 1762, learned dry-needle etching in Paris under Jacques-Philippe Le Bas in 1770-71, and then directed the academy for forty-three years -- the man who turned his master's book into a school, and whose roughly 2,500 prints are mostly book illustrations, portraits and city views. The birth date 19 June 1741 follows the scholarly biography (Carlien Jung, Biografisch Woordenboek van Nederland) and J. Knoef (1933); the Wikidata-derived infoboxes give 12 January 1741 (see C537). No words by Vinkeles survive except his letters to the Amsterdam bookseller and publisher Volkert van der Plaats, quoted here through BWN and Guido Jansen's transcription (see leg 1 F12).

**Doorway:** Hop from the Jan Punt study: Punt spent several years engraving his master's Rubens copies into copper and published them as a book, so the ceilings survived their fire twice over -- once in chalk, once in print; now meet the pupil who studied ten years with him, learned the dry needle in Paris, and then ran the drawing academy that taught from such books for forty-three years -- the school that is the print's afterlife, the institution Punt's pupil built out of his master's lessons. The doorway is the pupil who studied ten years with Punt and then ran the drawing academy.

One-hundred-and-sixty-fourth doorway check in a row. D1 "Reinier Vinkeles (1741-1816)" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (EN Wikipedia Reinier Vinkeles, 39 lines, read end to end by legs 1 and 3; BWN; Knoef; the years are undisputed -- only the day and month of birth are contested, see C537). D2 "the pupil who studied ten years with Punt" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (EN Wikipedia: "studied for some ten years with Jan Punt"; BWN: placed with Punt in 1752, aged eleven, independent c. 1762; Onno van Seggelen: assistant to Punt "for over a decade"). D3 "went to Paris in 1770 with Jacques-Philippe Le Bas, travelling with Jurriaan Andriessen, Izaak Schmidt and Hermanus Numan, and met Izaak Jansz de Wit (1744-1809)" -- HOLDS in substance and FAILS in the letter (Paris 1770 holds; Le Bas was his teacher there, not a travelling companion; Andriessen and Schmidt were the 1765 Brabant companions, not Paris travellers; Numan was met in Paris, not travelled with; de Wit was met in Paris -- see leg 1 F18). ## The life

Reinier Vinkeles was born in Amsterdam, the middle of three sons of Harmen Vinkeles, a middenstander (shopkeeper), and Elizabeth Prengers. The family lived in the Pijlsteeg, a street then known as a red-light street, and his father owned a rouwwinkel -- a shop hiring out funeral requisites such as mourning clothes and drinking vessels -- so the boy came into early contact with death (BWN/Jung). The birth date is carried here as 19 June 1741, following BWN and Knoef; the Wikidata-derived infoboxes give 12 January 1741, and even the Dutch Wikipedia page contradicts itself (infobox 12 January, body 19 June) -- see C537.

His parents intended him for trade (koophandel). The story goes that at primary school a classmate boasted about the drawing lessons he took and how difficult they were; Reinier wanted to prove it was not so hard, was shown the classmate's work and the object he had copied, tried the same example himself, and rendered it so accurately that everyone was impressed. The success stimulated him to practise drawing, after which his parents decided to have him learn engraving. BWN is explicit: "Of dit verhaal op waarheid berust, is onzeker" -- whether this story is true is uncertain. Knoef tells the same story more briefly: "een toeval openbaarde zijn aanleg voor het teekenen" -- a coincidence revealed his talent for drawing.

In 1752, aged eleven, Vinkeles was placed as a pupil with Jan Punt (1711-1779) -- in BWN's words "niet alleen de beroemdste toneelspeler van zijn tijd, maar ook een graveur van naam" (not only the most famous actor of his time, but also an engraver of repute). Punt also taught him the art of etching. By fifteen Vinkeles was already more collaborator than pupil of Punt, and after about ten years in his service he established himself as an independent engraver (c. 1762). EN Wikipedia says "studied for some ten years with Jan Punt"; Onno van Seggelen: "Reinier became the assistant to Punt for over a decade." Punt's own practice matters for understanding Vinkeles: Punt was a theatre man -- stage designer, engraver of theatre scenes and of Jacob de Wit's proportion book -- and Vinkeles inherited from him both the burin and the theatre.

In October 1762 Vinkeles became a member of the Amsterdam Stadstekenacademie (City Drawing Academy). After its reorganisation in 1765 he obtained a seat in the six-man directie (board of directors); another director was the architect Jacob Otten Husly (1738-1796). As a director he had to be present at the drawing sessions one month a year to give pupils directions and impart theoretical knowledge. He remained a director for 43 years; since 1805 he was the oldest in rank; in June 1808 he resigned the post and stayed on as an honorary member (BWN; NL Wiki).

His early masterpiece of institutional self-portraiture belongs to this world: the etching "The Drawing Academy at Amsterdam" (1764-68), an evening scene of a drawing session above the Leidse Poort with a male life model in a standard pose, signed, dated and lettered in Dutch with a description and dedication (V&A, E.288-1984, 34.6 x 39.2 cm). Knoef calls it the worthy opening of his non-book prints: in this evening scene "met verschillende kunstlichtbronnen" (with various artificial light sources) the engraver-draughtsman could fully indulge his treatment of artful contrasts of light and dark.

In 1765 he travelled to Brabant with Jurriaan Andriessen and Izaak Schmidt (EN Wiki; NL Wiki). The same year: his first portrait whose date is certain -- the great Amsterdam collector Gerrit Braamcamp, after Jacob Xavery -- and the triple portrait Portraits of Izaak Schmidt, Jurriaan Andriessen and Reinier Vinkeles (1765, Rijksmuseum). His earliest dated print is from 1762; the theatre illustration for Olympia dates to 1764 (Knoef). The coloured pendant views of the Amsterdam Theatre on the Keizersgracht -- figures arriving in daylight/summer, leaving at night/winter, canal right vs. left, viewpoint reversed -- exist in versions dated 1760, 1762 and 1765; Sotheby's notes the 1765 watercolour is one of the largest, that a pair dated 1762 is in the Amsterdam City Archives, and that a 1760 example made a record price for the artist in 2019 ($35,000, Sotheby's New York, 30 January 2019, lot 105). The theatre burned down in May 1772, twelve years after the 1760 sheet was drawn. FLAG: the 1760 date would make the earliest theatre views predate his independence (c. 1762) -- he was still Punt's assistant; plausible (he was already more collaborator than pupil at 15), but note the tension (leg 1 F17).

In 1770 Vinkeles left for Paris, where he studied for a year with the French engraver Jacques-Philippe Le Bas and met the Dutch artists Hermanus Numan and Izaak Jansz de Wit (1744-1809). Le Bas was known for his droge-naaldtechniek (dry-needle technique), with which the plate could be worked even more carefully; Vinkeles wanted to master this special method, was taken in among Le Bas's pupils and was even allowed to live in his house (BWN; NL Wiki; EN Wiki). During his stay in Paris the envoy of the Russian Empress Catherine II offered him a post as co-director (medebestuurder) of the Academy of Fine Arts in St Petersburg. He declined the honourable offer because he was too attached to his own country and family (BWN; Knoef: the offers came "van de zijde van Catharina II" while in Paris, and "de gehechtheid aan vaderland, familie en vrienden behield de overhand"). FLAG: NL Wikipedia places the invitation in 1771, the year of his return; BWN and Knoef place it during the Paris stay (leg 1 F3). And the brief's grouping -- that he "went to Paris in 1770 with Le Bas, travelling with Andriessen, Schmidt and Numan" -- merges three separate facts: the Brabant trip with Andriessen and Schmidt was 1765; in Paris he studied under Le Bas and met Numan and de Wit there (leg 1 F18; see C530).

In 1771 he returned to Amsterdam for good and began producing, in Knoef's words, "die overtalrijke prenten van alle soort, portretten, boekversiering, illustraties" -- countless prints of every kind -- alongside the drawings for them (where he did not work after others), co-management of the Amsterdam drawing academy, and the training of many pupils, until his death on 30 January 1816 (Knoef; NL Wiki). His output in the 1770s and 1780s was chiefly history and genre scenes. His prints have fine, well-considered compositions, and the scenes stand out for liveliness, depth and clarity. He was overloaded with commissions; although he took on pupils to help -- among them his sons Johannes and Abraham and his youngest brother Harmanus -- customers often had to wait long for their orders, which they gladly accepted, being willing to pay him a great deal of money for his work (BWN).

Key works and series of this middle period (all verified in sources read): theatre illustrations, often after his own drawings, e.g. for Lessing's Minna von Barnhelm (1780); stage designs with his brother Harmanus, e.g. Coulissendecor "Armoedige Wooning" (1777, after A. v.d. Groen); portraits after many painters: Sara Maria van der Wilp after D. Bruyninx (1772), N. Versteeg (1771), Petrus Bertius after J. Buys (1787), J. van Goudoever after J. Ekels, P. Broes (1792), Cornelis Ploos van Amstel after J. Buys (1799, after a 1766 portrait); book illustration: title page 1777; Reinhart by E. M. Post (1791); vignettes throughout; topography: Oost-Indisch Huis (1768); views "naer het leven" (from life), e.g. A view of the Oude Kerk, Amsterdam, seen from the Oudezijds Voorburgwal (black chalk, grey wash; Christie's) -- "Vinkeles specialised in drawing city views 'naer het leven' (from life). Part of the charm of Vinkeles' townscapes lies in the staffage; here, two men are looking at the Oude Kerk (which still stands today) in admiration"; history: De zelfopoffering van burgemeester Pieter van der Werff, 1574 (1774); murder of Johan van Oldebarnevelt (1777, for Wagenaar's Beknopte historie van 't vaderland); Overleg tussen zes voorname Amsterdammers, 1566 (1780-1795); the French Revolution series: Tafereelen van de Staatsomwenteling in Frankrijk (Amsterdam, Jan Allart, 1794-1807), 25 parts, 155 engraved plates (78 portraits, 77 double-page scenes) engraved by Vinkeles and his pupil Daniel Vrijdag after Brion, Duplessi-Bertaux, Ozanne, Vernet, Fragonard fils, Girodet, Swebach, etc.; plus The Trial of Louis XVI, engraved by Vinkeles and Vrijdag, 1802; Festival of Freedom on Dam Square, 4 March 1795, after Jacques Kuyper, with Vrijdag.

In 1781 Vinkeles, then almost forty, married the 27-year-old Catharina Jurgens (1753-1802); the ceremony (ondertrouw 25 May 1781) took place sixteen months after the birth of their first child. It was a happy marriage: between 1780 and 1794 the couple had four sons and three daughters. From the surviving correspondence with his friend the Amsterdam bookseller and publisher Volkert van der Plaats a cheerful husband and father emerges for these years (BWN).

From 1790 prints by him began to appear with less pure working; in this third period the difference between these careless engravings and his earlier work grew ever larger. After 1800 the technical decline was unmistakable: finer nuances were left out and the prints gave the impression of very hastily and roughly worked etchings. Criticism of his work increased, which he found unjust (BWN). From 1800 his letters grow less cheerful. On 19 April 1800 he wrote to Van der Plaats: "Man Lief onze kinderen beginnen ons te doen denken [dat] wij onze jaren krijgen" -- "My dear, our children are beginning to make us think [that] we are getting on in years"; "Oud worden foeij dat is leelijk" ("Growing old, fie, that is ugly"). His gloom increased; physically he was doing excellently, but mentally he felt steadily worse: "Ik voor mij doe alles noch met hetzelfde gemak als voor 25 jaaren", but he noted "minder lust" in life because "de Geestelijken Boosheid in de lugt los gebroken is" ("the Clerical Wickedness has broken loose in the air"). In June 1801 he reported his wife's health was very bad; he had to keep working and making plans despite "al die zorgelijke uitzichten" because he had seven children to support (BWN, all quotations via Guido Jansen, Jaarboek Amstelodamum 27 (1985) 107-121, pp. 113-115).

His wife died 21 April 1802: "Wat een gemis voor mij", he sighed to Van der Plaats on the day of her death. "Voortaan zal mijne woning eene spelonke der droefheid wesen. Het licht dat warmte en genoegen versprijde is uitgedoofd. Ruim drie en twintig Jaaren ben ik eene der gelukkigste stervelingen geweest, helaas geweest" ("Henceforth my home shall be a den of sorrow. The light that spread warmth and pleasure is extinguished. For well over twenty-three years I have been one of the happiest mortals -- alas, have been."). After Catharina's death he grew ever more dejected and saw his work only as a means of survival (BWN). To the criticism he answered on 16 September 1801: "de Posteriteyt zal kunnen oordeelen" ("posterity will be able to judge") that he could have made something more than little book prints, and that it was not his fault but that of the honoured public that he had to fritter away his time on the latter. Making simple illustrations for his bread he saw, in other words, as a waste of time; his talent reached further (BWN).

And yet the big plates he made around 1801 of the halls of Felix Meritis -- the society for the promotion of art and science on the Amsterdam Keizersgracht -- show this indeed. These engravings are the high point of his oeuvre, but he could not live from such work, to his regret (BWN; Knoef dates the two hall prints 1792 and 1794, after drawings by J. Kuiper and P. Barbiers, engraved with the help of N. v.d. Meer -- see C538. The Met dates its impression "ca. 1800", RISD dates its impression 1801.)

From 1797 to May 1812 he was a member of the literary society Concordia et Libertate, a closed, liberal-minded circle whose members entertained each other weekly in the winter months with lectures on their field of knowledge or interest. Vinkeles gave fifteen art lectures (kunstbeschouwingen) there over the years, discussing works by various masters and various engraving techniques (BWN). A great honour was his appointment by King Louis (Lodewijk Napoleon) on 6 July 1808 as a member of the Koninklijk Nederlandsch Instituut van Wetenschappen, Letterkunde en Schoone Kunsten (Royal Netherlands Institute of Sciences, Literature and Fine Arts), founded two months earlier. He sat in the Fourth Class, in which visual artists and musicians were united. On 22 August 1808 he was elected president of the Fourth Class -- the first official chairman for a term of six months. Afterwards he sat on various committees advising the King or his ministers on all kinds of art matters (BWN; DE Wiki confirms membership since 1808). He was also a member of the Amsterdam St Lucas guild (St. Luke's Guild) and of the artists' club Pax Artium Nutrix ("Peace feeds/nourishes art"), whose meetings took place at the Herberg de Zon inn on the Nieuwendijk -- many artists and art lovers frequented the inn, and Vinkeles's portrait of Anna de Jong, maid at the inn, records the milieu (NL Wiki; Pax search results). No source found calls him a founder of Pax Artium Nutrix -- see C531.

Late work was dominated by commissions for reference works: Kok's Vaderlandsch Woordenboek, the Vervolg van Wagenaar and similar publications, plus many city views and landscapes -- historically important rather than artistically at his best (NL Wiki). He also made the original drawings for the only two illustrations to Jacob Haafner's Lotgevallen op eene reize van Madras over Tranquebaar, naar het eiland Ceilon (Haarlem, A. Loosjes Pz., 1806): Onderhoud met den Jammedaar te Alamparve and the frontispiece showing Haafner with Anna landing on Caradiva. Haafner had been introduced to Vinkeles by the merchant J. Hilkes and was apprenticed in Vinkeles's workshop for a time (Onno van Seggelen).

In early 1816 Reinier Vinkeles died in Amsterdam at 74 after a short illness -- 30 January 1816. He left an extremely extensive oeuvre: the number of prints engraved by him is estimated at 2,500, of which 1,500 after his own drawings. Yet, despite the many words of praise after his death, he quickly fell into oblivion. What he left behind is nevertheless very special, because no North-Netherlandish engraver could or can equal the quality of his prints, and certainly not the enormous size of his oeuvre (BWN). His portrait was painted in oil by Charles Howard Hodges in 1816 (the year of his death; Rijksmuseum collection). His art collection was sold at auction in 1816 (catalogue in the RKD). His letters survive in the university libraries of Leiden and Amsterdam, the Royal Library in The Hague and the Amsterdam City Archives; the 18 letters to/from Volkert van der Plaats are transcribed in Jansen 1985, pp. 111-119 (BWN).

A contemporary obituary, "Levensschets van Reinier Vinkeles", appeared in the Algemeene Konst- en Letter-bode 11 (1816) 166-169 -- not read end to end in this leg (see leg 1 F12). The first full biographical treatment is Van Eynden and Van der Willigen, Geschiedenis der Vaderlandsche Schilderkunst II (1817) 316-325 and IV (1820) 182-183, which also supplied the 2,500-print estimate. The modern standard works are J. Knoef, "Het grafisch werk van Reinier Vinkeles", Elseviers Geillustreerd Maandschrift 43 (1933) 374-382, and C. M. Jung, Reinier Vinkeles (1741-1816). Leven en werk van de grootste achttiende-eeuwse graveur (unpublished doctoral thesis, Radboud University Nijmegen, 2005) and her BWN entry and Mededelingen van de Stichting Jacob Campo Weyerman 28 (2005) 118-133.

His pupils, as attested: Jacob Ernst Marcus (1774-1826) -- BWN notes Marcus's manner of handling derives from Vinkeles's late, looser manner; Jacobus Millies -- EN Wikipedia only (RKD), absent from DE Wikipedia and BWN lists (leg 1 F5); Abraham Vinkeles (son) and Johannes Vinkeles (son) -- assisted in the workshop; Harmanus (Harmen) Vinkeles (1745-1804) -- youngest brother, assisted, also made theatre sets jointly with Reinier; Daniel Vrijdag (1765-1822) -- co-engraver of the Revolution series and the Trial of Louis XVI; Jacob Haafner (1754-1809) -- briefly apprenticed in the workshop, not a formal pupil in the art-historical lists.

## M1. The burin line, hair-fine to boldly cut (the tonal scale)

Knoef's opening defence of engraving: "Een rijke verscheidenheid van tinten tusschen fluweelig zwart en het pure wit van het papier, een virtuoze hanteering van de lijn, van haar-dunne tot forsch-gekorvene" -- a rich variety of tints between velvety black and the pure white of the paper, a virtuoso handling of the line, from hair-thin to boldly incised. A single plate spans the full black-to-white scale purely by line weight and spacing; no wash, no colour. **Takeaway:** tone is a property of the line alone -- a form emerges from white paper through progressively denser hatching, and the burin is a complete tonal instrument.

## M2. Dry-needle etching (droge naald) learned from Le Bas

Le Bas was known for his dry-needle technique, "waarmee de plaat nog zorgvuldiger kon worden bewerkt" -- with which the plate could be worked even more carefully; Vinkeles went to Paris specifically to make this method his own, lived in Le Bas's house, and on his return showed he had mastered it (BWN). After 1771 the line turns softer and more delicate than burin work -- fine, slightly burr-softened strokes that hold ink differently, giving tender, precise forms with light tint gradations. FLAG: whether Le Bas's technique was true drypoint (direct needle on copper, with burr) or a related intaglio refinement is not resolved by these sources; the Dutch term is droge naald -- do not over-specify the tool (leg 1 F16). **Takeaway:** the Paris year is a second hand -- the same engraver returns with a softer line, and the plate can now hold both the crisp burin and the tender dry needle in one image.

## M3. Mixed intaglio: etched line + stipple + engraving

Knoef lists the "verwante technieken, van de geetste lijn, van de stippelmanier e.d." (related techniques: the etched line, the stipple manner, etc.) as part of the engraver's means, alongside the burin. Museum records confirm mixed technique: self-portrait "etching, engraving"; Gellert portrait 1775 "etching, engraving"; Ploos van Amstel "etching and engraving". Within one portrait, stippled dots build the soft modelling of a cheek while ruled burin lines build the hard edge of a coat or frame. **Takeaway:** the plate is a mixed register -- dot for flesh, line for cloth, and the engraver chooses the mark by the material it describes.

## M4. The three-period technical arc (the career as a visible curve)

BWN distinguishes three stages: (1) early -- follows Punt's classicist manner but with Simon Fokke's unbound, lively picturesque manner mixed in (some prints entirely in Fokke's painterly, mobile manner; others striving for an exact engraving, e.g. Olympia 1764); (2) c. 1770-1790 -- classicist drawing manner, "geheel volgens de heersende norm -- zorgvuldig gegraveerde prenten" among the best in the field, minute and subtle line, clear tender precise forms, light tint gradations against backgrounds "zoo ragfijn bewerkt dat ze op eenigen afstand effen lijken" (so finely worked that at some distance they seem even/flat); (3) from 1790, accelerating after 1800 -- less pure working, finer nuances omitted, prints like "zeer vluchtig en ruw opgewerkte etsen" (very hastily and roughly worked etchings) (BWN; Knoef). **Takeaway:** the same subject rendered in 1765, 1772 and 1800 is three different hands -- strong contrasts and large blank areas, then microscopic line, then loose sketchy strokes -- and the career is a time-lapse of a hand losing patience.

## M5. Portrait transposition: choosing the register for the sitter

Knoef: the draughtsman/painter determines the attitude of the work, "maar het verstaan en begripvol overzetten van diens bedoelingen berust bij den graveur en diens vermogen om aan te voelen met welke middelen en in welken trant hij zijn voorbeeld zal transponeeren" -- but understanding and sympathetically transposing the model's intentions rests with the engraver and his feeling for with which means and in which manner he will transpose his model. Across the career the portrait apparatus shrinks: early portraits use powerful but concise shadow indication beside large blank areas with a baroque flicker; by the 1770s the sitters appear in minute, subtle linework; by 1792 (P. Broes) the sitter stands "star-frontaal achter een toog" (stiffly frontal behind a counter), all accessory reduced to a single festoon or a bas-relief allusion to the sitter's profession set into the architectural frame; by the end of the century the sitter appears as a bust only, "ontdaan van elk sierend bijwerk" (stripped of all decorative accessory), in a frame of the simplest architecture (Knoef). **Takeaway:** the frame eats the portrait -- ornament drains out of the image decade by decade until only a bust in a plain rectangle remains, and the engraver's job is to decide how much frame a sitter deserves.

## M6. City view "naer het leven" with staffage as the looking device

Christie's: "Vinkeles specialised in drawing city views 'naer het leven' (from life). Part of the charm of Vinkeles' townscapes lies in the staffage; here, two men are looking at the Oude Kerk (which still stands today) in admiration." The same motif recurs in the Potasbranderij at the Anjeliersgracht drawing. The theatre pendants systematise it: arriving in daylight/summer with the canal at the right, leaving at night/winter with the canal at the left, viewed from the opposite direction -- the same building, two seasons, two directions, different staffage each time (Sotheby's). **Takeaway:** tiny figures inside the view do the looking for us -- they point, admire, enter, leave -- and the same facade re-drawn as a day/night, summer/winter pair turns a view into a system.

## M7. Theatre illustration as a light plot

Knoef on the theatre prints (often after his own drawing): high, strictly linear, sparsely furnished rooms, at first with a playful rocaille ornament lending a touch of dash, later even stricter; inside them the fates of the dramatis personae play out. The virtues are "zin voor de werkelijkheid, voor het karakteristieke, een geschoold oog voor de werkingen van het daglicht in een interieur, voor de kunstige effecten van andere lichtbronnen in het schaduwrijk donker" -- a sense of reality and the characteristic, a trained eye for the workings of daylight in an interior and for the artful effects of other light sources in shadow-rich darkness. The Minna von Barnhelm plate: figures in an extremely sober interior, flowed around by clear light falling through high windows that also give a view of the row of facades across the street, dissolving in strong outside light into a tender yet sharply defined silhouette; and by contrast the evening dark whose secrets torchlight or candlelight must try to penetrate (Knoef). **Takeaway:** the light source is the plot -- daylight through tall windows vs. a single candle in darkness -- and the figures are placed where the light falls.

## M8. The vignette and title page as a miniature composition

Book decoration occupied him his whole long life, after his own or others' designs: "Gracieuze niemendalletjes, de attributen der poezie, luchtig met linten omstrikt, wat korenaren en een bloem, dan ook weer zorgvuldige, afgeronde composities in het klein, heel vaak, vooral later, groepen van putti" -- graceful trifles, the attributes of poetry lightly bound with ribbons, some ears of corn and a flower, then again careful rounded compositions in miniature, very often, especially later, groups of putti. Later title pages become classical-strict, enlivened only by a sober portrait medallion, a lyre, a Roman lamp. Knoef notes the putti's weak drawing (neither quite little angels nor quite earthly children, "slap-bolle lichamen en leden" -- slackly bulging bodies and limbs) and excuses it: the vignette fashion had already passed in France by the mid-1770s, so Amsterdam had to live on old compositional elements. One vignette known only in a proof before lettering shows, in the smallest compass, a landscape with distant hills and a waterfall, herbs and undergrowth in the foreground, a plain rectangular stone enclosing a deepened oval with a relief of the attributes of one of the arts -- "Hellas' stille grootheid" (the silent grandeur of Hellas) in miniature (Knoef). **Takeaway:** a whole landscape with waterfall and monument compressed into a two-inch tailpiece -- the vignette is a landscape that fits on a page of text, and its smallness is the skill.

## M9. The design-to-plate pipeline with collaborators and proof states

Vinkeles "meermalen ook de teekeningen maakte waarnaar hij werkte" (often made the drawings he worked from) -- but equally worked after others, above all Jacobus Buys, whose drawings underlie countless illustrations; and he was often assisted by other engravers (N. v.d. Meer on the Felix Meritis halls; Daniel Vrijdag on the Revolution series), while the underlying drawings were "in de meeste gevallen niet van de eerste orde" (in most cases not of the first order). Proof states matter: a vignette survives only as a "druk voor den letter" (print before the lettering), revealing neither maker's name nor destination (Knoef). The credit line migrates between draughtsman, engraver and publisher (e.g. Met trade card: engraver Vinkeles, artist J. G. Waldorp, publisher C. S. Roos). **Takeaway:** the same image exists in states -- before lettering, after lettering -- and the plate is a relay, not a single hand: design, engraving, assistance, imprint.

## M10. Artificial-light set pieces (evening academies)

The 1768 Drawing Academy etching is an evening scene "met verschillende kunstlichtbronnen" (with various artificial light sources) in which the engraver-draughtsman could fully indulge his treatment of artful contrasts of light and dark, "zoo goed passend bij zijn toenmaligen picturalen trant" (so well suited to his then pictorial manner). The Felix Meritis hall prints do the same for public interiors: the physics hall (1794/1801) with its electrical machine, the music hall and auditorium with seated Amsterdam burghers diverting themselves (Knoef; RISD/Met records). **Takeaway:** a roomful of people arranged around a single lit object -- a life model, an electrical machine, a music stand -- with the light falling off into darkness at the edges; the academy is a stage lit by one lamp.

## M11. Topographical and historical documentation as a parallel oeuvre

Beyond the book: war scenes on land and sea, depictions of all kinds of events, views of foreign regions, reproductions after old masters, theatre prints -- much of it made with less than his full attention, often with help, after second-rate drawings, yet rising at times to surprising heights. His later work for Kok's Vaderlandsch Woordenboek, the Vervolg van Wagenaar and similar works, plus his many city views and landscapes, is of historical importance (Knoef; NL Wiki). **Takeaway:** the same hand that engraved a portrait with microscopic care also turned out a double-page Revolution scene or a foreign view -- the documentary plate is a genre with its own looser, flatter register, and it is the bulk of the 2,500.

## M12. Teaching by presence and by lecture

As academy director he was present one month a year at the drawing sessions to instruct pupils and teach theory (BWN). The Felix Meritis drawing academy he engraved (1801) was "outfitted for study from live models"; students "honed their skills in depicting human anatomy by drawing from both a live model and a skinned cadaver" (RISD). At Concordia et Libertate he gave fifteen kunstbeschouwingen (art lectures) discussing works by various masters and various engraving techniques (BWN). **Takeaway:** the academy is a staged room -- model on a platform, students in a ring, a flayed cadaver beside the living body -- and the lecture is a second medium: the engraver talking about other engravers' techniques, the school teaching itself out loud.

## Works

## W1. A View of the Keizersgracht in Amsterdam, with Figures Leaving the Stadsschouwburg Theatre (night scene), 1760

Date 1760 (signed and dated "R. Vinkeles Delineavit 1760", lower left). Medium pen and black ink and grey wash, with watercolour, over traces of an underdrawing in black chalk. Dimensions 277 x 434 mm -- reported-but-unverified (Stephen Ongpin search snippet; the dealer page read end to end, 30 lines, does not state dimensions in its extracted text). Current location private collection, USA (provenance on the Ongpin page); formerly on the London art market via Stephen Ongpin Fine Art. What it shows mechanically: the night-time pendant of the Schouwburg pair -- crowds leaving the Amsterdam theatre in winter, bare trees, the canal at the left, the viewpoint reversed against the daylight arrival scene; a poster on the wall advertises Corneille's Le Cid. Vinkeles at 19, before he joined the Stadstekenacademie (1762), already working in colour ad vivum on a large sheet and already repeating a successful motif in multiple finished versions. The dealer text calls it "one of the earliest and largest" of his theatre drawings, and the biographer Adriaan van der Willigen (1817) singled out exactly these coloured theatre-entry/exit drawings as the best of Vinkeles's early work. It establishes the two habits that run through his whole career: the crowd as staffage animating a topographical view, and the same composition reworked at several scales.

## W2. View of the Keizersgracht: Visitors Entering / Leaving the Amsterdam Theatre (van Vlissingen pair), 1760 (night) / 1761 (daylight)

Date 1760 (night scene) and 1761 (daylight scene), both signed and dated; inscribed "Gezigt van de Keyzers graft & Amsteldamsche Schouberg 1760". Medium watercolour (pair of watercolours). Dimensions 185 x 250 mm each -- reported-but-unverified (stated on the Ongpin page read end to end, lines 21-24, and corroborated by Sotheby's catalogue text; Ongpin is a dealer, not a museum or auction house, so per the #533 definition this is not VERIFIED). Current location John and Marine van Vlissingen Art Foundation, Netherlands (acquired Paris, Tajan, 18 May 1999, lot 115); illustrated in Home and Abroad (Rijksmuseum / Fondation Custodia, 2015-16), pp. 188-192, nos. 81a and 81b. What it shows mechanically: the small-format version of the same pendant pair as W1 -- daylight arrival in summer, trees in full leaf, canal at right, artist looking north; night departure in winter, canal at left, viewed from the opposite direction. The pair proves the motif was a repeatable product, not a one-off: Vinkeles made "several finished versions ... of various sizes and each with different staffage". At 19-20 he is already a topographical entrepreneur, selling Amsterdam to Amsterdam. The day/night, summer/winter inversion -- same architecture, flipped light, season and direction -- is the earliest clear instance of his systematic variation, a device he later applies to history (same event, new plate) rather than to place.

## W3. Afbeelding der Teeken Academie, zoo als Dezelve tot den Jaare 1767 gehouden werd boven het Corps de Garde van de Leydsche Poort te Amsterdam (The Drawing Academy at Amsterdam), drawn 1764, engraved 1768

Date 1764-68 (V&A brief description). Plate lettering reads "R. Vinkeles ad vivum Delin 1764 et Sculp 1768" -- drawn from life 1764, engraved 1768 (see leg 2 flag 1). Medium etching on paper. Dimensions height 34.6 cm x width 39.2 cm -- VERIFIED (V&A page read end to end, 47 lines; "Note: Taken from register"). Current location Victoria and Albert Museum, London, Prints, Drawings and Paintings Collection, accession E.288-1984. What it shows mechanically: a night piece -- the interior of the Stadstekenacademie above the Corps de Garde at the Leidsepoort, students ranged around a raised platform where a male life model holds a standard pose. Vinkeles drew the room he himself had joined in 1762, then engraved it four years later -- a self-portrait of his institution as much as a print. It is the hinge of his early career: the apprentice (Punt's assistant for over a decade) picturing the academy that will later make him a director. The etched caption and dedication in Dutch make it a public document, not a study sheet. A second impression was offered by Bubb Kuyper, 17 May 2024, described as "Splendid night piece", 33.4 x 37.1 cm, provenance A. M. van den Broek -- a slightly different measurement for what may be another impression or a trimmed sheet (leg 2 flag 1).

## W4. The Keizersgracht, Amsterdam, with the entrance to the Schouwburg (daylight arrival), 1765

Date 1765 (signed and dated in black ink, lower right: "R. Vinkeles ad viv delin 1765"). Medium pen and grey ink and watercolour, over pencil, within brown ink framing lines. Dimensions 273 x 398 mm -- reported-but-unverified (Sotheby's/Invaluable search snippet; the Sotheby's 2024 page read end to end, 32 lines, does not state dimensions in its extracted text). Current location private collection; offered Sotheby's, New York, Old Master and British Works on Paper, 2024 (estimate $4,000-$6,000); an earlier example of the same view sold Sotheby's New York, 30 January 2019, lot 105, $35,000 (a record price for the artist at the time). What it shows mechanically: the daylight pendant again, now at near-W1 scale and dated 1765 -- summer, full leaf, canal at right, crowds arriving at the Schouwburg entrance. Sotheby's calls it "one of the largest of a number of drawings ... of figures outside the theater". By 1765 Vinkeles is 24, has travelled to Brabant with Jurriaan Andriessen and Izaak Schmidt, and is producing the theatre views as finished, saleable watercolours rather than sketches. The repetition across 1760, 1761, 1762 (Amsterdam City Archives pair, Atlas Splitberger 481-482) and 1765 shows a young artist who has found a marketable formula and is working it -- scale up, staffage varied, same architecture. FLAG: Sotheby's 2024 text says "twelve years after the present sheet was drawn, the theatre was completely destroyed by fire" (1772) -- twelve years fits a 1760 sheet, not a 1765 one; the sentence is carried over from the 1760 lot description (Sotheby's 2019, lot 105) -- a copy error (leg 2 flag 3).

## W5. Portretten van Izaak Schmidt, Jurriaan Andriessen en Reinier Vinkeles (Portraits of Izaak Schmidt, Jurriaan Andriessen and Reinier Vinkeles), 1765

Date 1765. Medium engraving. Dimensions height 167 mm x width 111 mm -- reported-but-unverified (Artera search snippet, mirroring Rijksmuseum data). Current location Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam (object number not given in the snippet). What it shows mechanically: an allegorical group portrait -- the three young artists, Schmidt, Andriessen and Vinkeles himself, represented not directly but through an allegory in which a cupid, brush in hand, performs the labour of portraiture. Made the year of the Brabant trip with the same two companions, it is a friendship print and a professional manifesto: three twenty-somethings picturing themselves as a circle. It also shows Vinkeles already engraving portraits in 1765, the skill that will become his bread and butter in book illustration. FLAG: a print seller (Meisterdrucke/Bridgeman) attributes this image to Jurriaan Andriessen, while the Rijksmuseum attributes it to Vinkeles -- the engraver is disputed (leg 2 flag 5).

## W6. Oost-Indisch Huis (East India House, Amsterdam), 1768

Date 1768. Medium engraving/etching (print; medium not precisely stated in sources consulted). Dimensions NOT VERIFIED (no dimensions found). Current location not stated in sources consulted (image reproduced from Amsterdam Bureau Monumenten and Archeologie, bma.amsterdam.nl, via Wikipedia). What it shows mechanically: a topographical view of the VOC headquarters on the Kloveniersburgwal -- the gate with Tuscan pilasters, the tunnel, the inner courtyard in Amsterdam Renaissance style. It is the best-known single image in the Wikipedia gallery for Vinkeles and the type-specimen of his architectural print: a civic monument rendered with the same crowd-and-facade grammar as the theatre views, but now in the service of institutional Amsterdam rather than the stage. Dated 1768, it belongs to the moment before Paris (1770) and shows the engraver's line already capable of carrying architecture without colour. Its fame is partly secondary -- it illustrates the building's Wikipedia article -- which is itself a measure of how Vinkeles's topographical prints became the visual record of the city. FLAG: the 1768 date and the image come from the building's Wikipedia article, sourced to Amsterdam Bureau Monumenten and Archeologie; no museum object page for this print was found in this leg -- date and medium should be re-verified against the Rijksmuseum collection before reuse (leg 2 flag 13).

## W7. Briefschrijvende man onder toeziend oog van geestelijken (Man writing a letter under the watchful eye of clergymen), 1768

Date 1768. Medium engraving. Dimensions height 172 mm x width 128 mm -- reported-but-unverified (Artera search snippet, mirroring Rijksmuseum data). Current location Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam. What it shows mechanically: a small narrative interior -- a man writing a letter while clergymen look on. The subject is literary and anecdotal rather than topographical -- a scene from a book, or a book illustration in search of a text. It marks the turn, visible from 1768-69, from the drawn view toward the engraved book plate: small format (roughly postcard size), dense incident, legible at arm's length. The Artera commentary stresses that such prints were "reproduced en masse" and brought Vinkeles "both income and a voice" -- the economics of the book trade replacing the economics of the single coloured drawing.

## W8. Vignette, illustration to Jacob Campo Weyerman, De levens-beschryvingen der Nederlandsche konst-schilders en konst-schilderessen, vol., p. 342, 1769

Date 1769 (inscribed "R. Vinkeles sculp. 1769"). Medium etching on paper; print, book-illustration. Dimensions height 74 mm x width 113 mm -- reported-but-unverified (British Museum search snippet; the BM object page itself returned only boilerplate and was not counted as read). Current location British Museum, London, Prints and Drawings, registration number 1874,0613.2437 (purchased from George E. Mason). What it shows mechanically: a vignette -- figures outside an enclosure wall, a town beyond, set within a decorative frame -- as a head- or tail-piece for Weyerman's lives of Dutch painters (Dordrecht, published by Abraham Blusse, 4 vols., 1769). At 74 x 113 mm it is the smallest work in the twenty, and the point is the smallness: Vinkeles is now a jobbing book engraver, fitting landscape, figure and ornament into a vignette that must sit quietly on a page of text. The decorative frame is doing compositional work the image alone cannot -- a miniaturist discipline opposite to the big Schouwburg sheets. It also fixes his position in the trade: illustrating other men's books, after other men's designs, for a Dordrecht publisher.

## W9. Inkomst van stadhouder Willem V en Wilhelmina van Pruisen te Amsterdam (Entry of Stadholder Willem V and Wilhelmina of Prussia into Amsterdam), 1772

Date 1772. Medium engraving (print; medium not precisely stated in the Delpher listing). Dimensions NOT VERIFIED. Current location not stated; listed in Delpher / Geheugen van Nederland under "Engravings and drawings from the eighteenth century" (four duplicate entries, same title and date). What it shows mechanically: a ceremonial entry print -- the stadholder and his Prussian wife arriving in Amsterdam, the city receiving its prince. This is Vinkeles as civic reporter -- the topographical eye turned to a dated public event, the kind of print that sells because the event is news. It sits between the theatre views and the later history prints: same crowd choreography, but the crowd is now a political public, and the architecture is a backdrop to power rather than the subject. The duplication in the Delpher index (four entries) hints at multiple impressions or states circulating, typical of event prints. It also prefigures the 1790s: the artist who records the stadholder's entry will spend the next decades recording the revolutions that unseat him. FLAG: Delpher duplicates are duplicate index entries, not separate works (leg 2 flag 14).

## W10. Afbeelding van het coulissendecor "De Italiaansche Straat" met scene uit De wedergevonden zoon (Image of the wing set "The Italian Street", with a scene from The Son Found Again), 1775

Date 1775. Medium print/engraving of a theatre set design (coulissendecor); medium not precisely stated in the Delpher listing. Dimensions NOT VERIFIED. Current location not stated; Delpher / Geheugen van Nederland, collection "A look at theatre" (Een kijk op theater). What it shows mechanically: a wing set -- a painted street in Italianate perspective -- with a scene from the play De wedergevonden zoon (The Son Found Again) staged inside it. This is the first of the theatre-set prints in the twenty and a change of viewpoint: from the street outside the Schouwburg (W1/W2/W4) to the stage itself, from spectator to scenographer. Vinkeles and his brother Harmanus (1745-1804) made a series of these set designs after other designers, engraving architecture that was itself painted scenery -- a print of a painting of a street. It is also the most theatrical of his documentary modes: the set is a fiction, but the engraving records it as if it were a place, with the same perspectival discipline as the Keizersgracht views.

## W11. Coulissendecor "Het Bosch" met scene uit Don Quichot op de bruiloft van Kamacho (Pieter Langendijk) (Wing set "The Wood", with a scene from Don Quixote at Camacho's Wedding), 1776

Date 1776. Medium print/engraving of a theatre set design; made by R. and H. Vinkeles after A. v. d. Groen. Dimensions NOT VERIFIED. Current location not stated; Delpher / Geheugen van Nederland, collection "A look at theatre". Also listed in Google Arts and Culture as Toneeldecor: Het Bosch, dated 1776. What it shows mechanically: a woodland wing set with a scene from Langendijk's Don Quixote play -- Don Quixote at Camacho's wedding -- staged inside painted trees. Made jointly with his brother Harmanus after Adolf van der Groen, it shows the family workshop in operation and the division of labour Vinkeles will repeat for decades: another man's design, the Vinkeles brothers' engraving. The subject is comic and literary (Cervantes via a Dutch stage adaptation), a long way from the civic solemnity of W9. Two Delpher entries exist for this set (one credited to Vinkeles, one to Groen), which is a cataloguing split, not two works (leg 2 flag 14). Together W10-W12 form a tight 1775-77 cluster: three set designs in three years, all for the Amsterdam Schouwburg, all collaborative.

## W12. Coulissendecor "Armoedige Wooning" met scene uit De Deugdzaame Armoede (L.S. Mercier) (Wing set "Poor Dwelling", with a scene from Virtuous Poverty), 1777

Date 1777. Medium print/engraving of a theatre set design; by R. and H. Vinkeles after A. v. d. Groen. Dimensions NOT VERIFIED. Current location not stated; Delpher / Geheugen van Nederland, collection "A look at theatre" (two duplicate entries, same title and date). What it shows mechanically: a poor dwelling -- a humble interior as painted scenery -- with a scene from Mercier's De Deugdzaame Armoede (Virtuous Poverty). The set is the opposite of W10's Italian street: not a grand perspectival vista but a cramped, low room, and the play is a sentimental drama about poverty with dignity. Vinkeles engraves a set that asks the audience to look closely at small things, and the print does the same work for the reader at home. It closes the 1775-77 theatre-set cluster and shows his range within a single commission type: street, wood, hovel; comedy, romance, moral drama. The collaboration credit "R. en H. Vinkeles" is now routine -- the brothers as a firm.

## W13. Portrait of the cartographer Petrus Bertius (after Jacobus Buys), 1787

Date 1787. Medium copper engraving on paper; oval bust portrait, after J. Buys (Jacobus Buys). Dimensions NOT VERIFIED (no dimensions found in sources consulted). Current location not stated; sold at auction (H. W. Fichter Kunsthandel, via Invaluable). What it shows mechanically: an oval bust of Petrus Bertius (1565-1629), cartographer and librarian, in ruff and doublet, after a design by Jacobus Buys, signed in the plate. Bertius had been dead for 150 years: this is a historical portrait, engraved for a biographical dictionary, not a likeness from life. It is the clearest example of Vinkeles's 1780s portrait factory: Buys designs, Vinkeles engraves, the publisher (Jan Allart and others) reuses the plate across dictionaries and histories. The Van Someren catalogue (1888) records dozens of such Buys/Vinkeles portraits, many reused in Chalmot's Biographisch woordenboek (1798-1800), Kok's Vaderlandsch woordenboek and Wagenaar's Vervolg. The work is small, standardised and repeatable -- the opposite of the unique Schouwburg watercolours, and the economic base of his middle career.

## W14. Illustratie voor het toneelstuk Het mislukt bedrog van F.W. Gotter (Illustration for the play The Failed Deception, by F. W. Gotter), 1791

Date 1791. Medium etching, ink and pen drawing on paper. Dimensions NOT VERIFIED. Current location Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam. What it shows mechanically: a stage illustration for Gotter's comedy Het mislukt bedrog (The Failed Deception / The Frustrated Deception): a man entering at the right -- read by the Artera commentary as a patriarchal figure embodying traditional values under challenge -- inside a drawn interior. After the 1770s set designs (W10-W12), which recorded scenery, this records the play itself: actors, gesture, moral comedy. The medium note matters -- "etching, ink, and pen drawing" -- suggesting a worked-up drawing as much as a print, and a return to the hand after years of reproductive engraving. It is also a late-Enlightenment artefact: theatre as a place of social debate, the print as affordable art carrying that debate home.

## W15. Zware mist te Amsterdam, 1790 (Heavy fog in Amsterdam, 1790), made 1791-1795

Date subject 1790; made 1791-1795. Medium print, engraving. Dimensions height 145 mm x width 110 mm -- reported-but-unverified (Artera page read end to end, 83 lines, gives the dimensions; Artera is an AI-curated mirror of Rijksmuseum data, not the museum itself, so per the #533 definition this is not VERIFIED). Current location Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam. What it shows mechanically: a small, near-monochrome cityscape in which Amsterdam all but disappears -- buildings recede into haze, a bridge, a few figures in darkness, light from a handful of lamps. The Artera commentary reads it as Dutch Romanticism and the sublime -- hatching and cross-hatching used not to describe architecture but to dissolve it. After thirty years of crisp topographical line, Vinkeles is engraving atmosphere itself, and the print is valued for what it withholds. It is the most "modern" feeling work in the twenty: a fog piece, an exercise in low visibility, made at the exact moment (1791-95) when his other hand is busy with the hard-edged reportage of the French Revolution (W17). The two modes -- fog and fact -- run side by side in the same years. FLAG: the title says 1790; Artera dates the making to 1791-1795 -- the 1790 is the depicted fog event (or the subject date), not the engraving date (leg 2 flag 8).

## W16. Zondvloed (The Deluge), 1793

Date 1793. Medium engraving. Dimensions NOT VERIFIED (the Artera page read end to end, 48 lines, gives no dimensions). Current location Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam. What it shows mechanically: the biblical Flood -- rising waters, terrified figures, some clinging to rocks in the foreground, smaller figures drowning behind, a waterfall cascading into the flood. The Artera commentary stresses "very fine lines to build form", gradations of light to dark, and a foreground/background contrast of anguish against drowning. It is a history print without a date or a place -- pure narrative catastrophe -- and it belongs with the flood and disaster imagery that runs through his 1790s work (compare the later Verwoesting ... te Beusichem, 1809, in Beyond the 20). Where W15 dissolves the city in fog, W16 drowns the world in water: the engraver testing how far line alone can carry terror. It also shows the biblical print as a steady seller alongside the news print -- the same hand, the same market, different dread.

## W17. Plegtige Omgang van de drie Standen te Versailles, den 4den van Bloeimaand 1789 (Procession of the Three Estates to Versailles, 4 May 1789), engraved 1794

Date lettered "J. Bulthuis, delin. R. Vinkeles & D. Vrydag, sculp. 1794". Series: Tafereelen van de Staatsomwenteling in Frankrijk (Pictures from the French Revolution). Medium etching, printing ink on paper. Dimensions height 22.23 cm x width 26.35 cm -- VERIFIED (V&A page read end to end, 58 lines). Current location Victoria and Albert Museum, London, Prints, Drawings and Paintings Collection, accession E.259-1953. What it shows mechanically: the ceremonial procession of the Three Estates to Versailles on the eve of the Revolution -- clergy, nobility and commons in ordered ranks, a crowd scene on a historic scale. Designed by Jan Bulthuis, engraved jointly by Vinkeles and his pupil Daniel Vrijdag. This is the first Tafereelen plate in the twenty and the start of the great collaborative enterprise of Vinkeles's later career: 25 parts issued by Jan Allart, Amsterdam, 1794-1807, eventually some 155 plates. The Amsterdam crowd of W1 has become a French crowd, and the topographical view has become newsreel: the print is dated five years after the event it depicts, engraved in Amsterdam from French designs, for a Dutch audience watching the Revolution unfold. The pupil is now a partner -- Vrijdag's name stands beside his master's in the lettering.

## W18. Voorvallen te Vincennes, op den 28sten van Sprokkelmaand van 1791 (The Parisians attacking the Chateau of Vincennes, 28 February 1791), engraved 1798

Date lettered "J. Bulthuis, delin. R. Vinkeles & D. Vrydag, sculp. 1798". Series: Tafereelen van de Staatsomwenteling in Frankrijk. Medium etching, printing ink on paper. Dimensions height 22.23 cm x width 25.08 cm -- VERIFIED (V&A page read end to end, 53 lines). Current location Victoria and Albert Museum, London, Prints, Drawings and Paintings Collection, accession E.293-1953. What it shows mechanically: a crowd assaulting the Chateau of Vincennes -- the ordered procession of W17 has become a mob at a fortress gate. Same designers and engravers (Bulthuis delin., Vinkeles and Vrijdag sculp.), same series, but the subject has tipped from ceremony into violence, and the seven-year gap between event (1791) and engraving (1798) shows the series working backwards through the Revolution as material arrived. The plate is one of 81 Vinkeles/Vrijdag etchings from the series held by the V&A (the V&A's count; the full series runs to about 155 plates -- see leg 2 flag 6). Vinkeles is now 57, engraving other men's designs of events he never saw, in a production line with his former pupil. The crowd choreography he learned on the Keizersgracht is now the visual language of revolution.

## W19. The Drawing Academy at the association of artists Felix Meritis in Amsterdam (Teken zaal in het gebouw der Maatschappy Felix Meritis binnen Amsterdam), 1801

Date 1801 (RISD). Published by C. S. Roos, Amsterdam, (1802) according to the Bubb Kuyper catalogue; "ca. 1800" in print-seller listings (see leg 2 flag 2). Medium etching and engraving on laid paper, after Pieter Barbiers and Jacques Kuyper; engraved by Reinier Vinkeles and Daniel Vrijdag (RISD tombstone: "After Pieter Barbiers ... After Jacques Kuyper"). Dimensions sheet 46 x 57.5 cm (18 1/8 x 22 5/8 in.) -- VERIFIED (RISD Museum page read end to end, 101 lines). Current location RISD Museum, Providence, Rhode Island, accession 2008.16.3 (purchased in honour of Roger Mandle, RISD President 1993-2008; Helen M. Danforth Acquisition Fund). A second impression, about 42 x 54 cm, was offered by Bubb Kuyper, 17 May 2024, with about 20 other Amsterdam views (estimate 100-150 euros). What it shows mechanically: the drawing academy again -- but thirty-seven years after W3, and a different academy: the custom-built drawing hall of the Felix Meritis society, organised around a stage with a wire frame above from which ropes suspend a model's arms in a desired pose, students drawing from a live model and a skinned cadaver (ecorche). It is the largest sheet in the twenty (46 x 57.5 cm) and the most architectural of the academy images: the room itself is the subject, a machine for looking. Vinkeles, now 60 and a director of the old Stadstekenacademie, engraves the new society's hall after Barbiers and Kuyper, with Vrijdag. The pair W3/W19 brackets his career: the young academician drawing his own school by night; the old master engraving another society's purpose-built hall in the full light of a professional commission. The life model is still there, in a standard pose, on a stage -- the one constant.

## W20. Onderhoud met den Jammedaar te Alamparve (Jacob Haafner conversing with the "Jammedaar" in Alamparve, Ceylon), and Jacob Haafner with Anna and the two girls land on the island Caradiva (frontispiece design), 1806 (book)

Date 1806 (the book: J. Haafner, Lotgevallen op eene reize van Madras over Tranquebaar, naar het eiland Ceilon, A. Pz. Loosjes, Haarlem, 1806). The two drawings are the preparatory designs for the book's only two illustrations. Medium drawings (preparatory designs for engraved plates; medium not precisely stated on the dealer page -- pen/ink/wash not specified). The engraved plates are Onderhoud met den Jammedaar te Alamparve and the title-vignette/frontispiece. Dimensions NOT VERIFIED (the dealer page read end to end, 70 lines, gives no dimensions). Current location private (offered as a notable sale by Onno van Seggelen, Amsterdam). What it shows mechanically: two exotic scenes from Jacob Haafner's Ceylon travels -- in the first, Haafner, summoned by the jammedaar's soldiers the moment he lands, visits the local captain in Alamparve, pretending to be a French ambassador until found out; in the second, Haafner and Anna (the "mestiezenmeisje" with whom he spent, in the dealer's words, "the most sensational hours of his life") land on Caradiva and are escorted by locals to a water well. Haafner had been a pupil in Vinkeles's workshop, introduced by the merchant J. Hilkes. The drawings are the last works in the twenty and the furthest travelled: Amsterdam topographical habit applied to Ceylon, a Dutch engraver imagining a coastline he never saw from a returned traveller's account. The dealer notes that exotic and colonial drawings by Vinkeles are "exceedingly rare" on the market, the previous one offered in 2002 (Sotheby's Amsterdam, 5 November 2002, lot 172, Horse and his Creole Jockey). At 65, Vinkeles is still a book illustrator -- but the book is now a travel memoir by a colonial critic (Haafner called the European discovery of India "the biggest disaster this country has been able to face"), and the image is a collaboration across a life: master and former pupil, Amsterdam and Ceylon.

## Beyond the 20

- **Self-portrait** (etching and engraving, oval bust, wig and robe; inscribed "Rein. Vinkeles. ad vivum del. et Sculp."). British Museum, reg. 1867,1214.801, purchased from John Deffett Francis, 1867. Production date given as the unhelpful range "1764-1816" (leg 2 flag 10) -- it cannot be placed chronologically and is therefore in Beyond the 20, not in W1-W20.
- **Poort van het Rasphuis** / *Gate of the Rasphuis* (prison gate, a prisoner being brought in in handcuffs). Undated in the Wikipedia gallery; the Rasphuis was a prison/poorhouse on the Heiligeweg, closed 1815, demolished 1892.
- **A bull and sheep in a landscape** (1765). Listed by WahooArt as the artist's single catalogued "painting"; not verified against a museum source -- treat as unconfirmed.
- **Reclining Man Smoking a Pipe** -- pen and brown ink and wash, watercolour over black chalk, traces of graphite on laid paper; sheet 14.9 x 19.4 cm. Art Gallery of Ontario (image-licensing detail report). A small figure study, useful evidence of his wash handling outside the theatre views.
- **Trade Card** (engraving, 19th century). Metropolitan Museum of Art, object 26.28.313, gift of Bella C. Landauer, 1926; housed in an album of British trade cards. Ephemera -- the engraver as jobbing commercial artist.
- **Portrait of Ploos van Amstel** (after Cornelis Ploos van Amstel, possibly by Reinier Vinkeles), 1799. Etching and engraving; Davison Art Center, Wesleyan University, acc. 1947.D1.197.51. Attribution is "possibly by" (leg 2 flag 12) -- do not present it as a certain Vinkeles.
- **The Trial of Louis XVI before the Convention, 26 December 1792** (engraved by Vinkeles and Daniel Vrijdag, 1802; after Miller). Tafereelen series; coloured engraving; Bibliotheque Nationale, Paris (via Bridgeman).
- **The attack on the Tuileries Palace, 10 August 1792** (lettered "Monnet, delin. R. Vinkeles en D. Vrydag, sculp. 1800"). Etching; V&A, E.321-1953; dimensions height 22.54 cm x width 36.35 cm -- VERIFIED (V&A page read end to end, 56 lines). The widest Tafereelen sheet in this leg.
- **The noyades (rebels being drowned) in the Loire at Nantes, December 1793** (lettered "Duplessis Bertaux, del. R. Vinkeles en D. Vrijadg, sculp. 1804" -- sic). Etching; V&A, E.371-1953; height 21.59 cm x width 25.4 cm -- VERIFIED (V&A page read end to end, 58 lines).
- **The opening of the States General at Versailles, 5 May 1789** (lettered "J. Bukthuis, delin. R. Vinkeles & D. Vrydag. sculp. 1794"). Etching; V&A, E.260-1953. Tafereelen series.
- **The discovery of Louis XVI at Varennes, 22 June 1791** (lettered "J. Bulthuis, delin. R. Vinkeles & D. Vrijdag, sculp. 1799"). Etching; V&A, E.303-1953. Tafereelen series.
- **Massacre of villagers of La Chapelle, on the St. Denis road, by soldiers, January 1791** (lettered "J. Bulthuis, delin. R. Vinkeles & D. Vrijdag. sculp. 1798"). Etching; V&A, E.297-1953. Tafereelen series.
- **De eerste zitting van de Nationale Vergadering** / *The first sitting of the National Assembly*, 1796 (R. Vinkeles en D. Vrijdag). Delpher, "Engravings and drawings from the eighteenth century".
- **Moord op Willem van Oranje, 1584** (1782-84). Rijksmuseum. Dutch-history print, same series habit as W13's historical portraits.
- **Aanval van de Britten op Lemmer, 1799** (1800-1802); **Storm op het IJ, 1788** (1788-1795); **Schipbreuk van de Overhout, 1777** (1780-1795, after Jacobus Buys). Rijksmuseum. Maritime/disaster prints.
- **Haremvrouw B** (1803-1805). Engraving, height 192 mm x width 120 mm (Artera snippet) -- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED. Rijksmuseum. Medium note is confused in the source ("pencil drawn sketch" vs "engraving") -- see leg 2 flag 9.
- **Man richt zijn wapen op een andere man** / *Man aims his weapon at another man* (1808-1809). Engraving, height 250 mm x width 167 mm (Artera snippet) -- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED. Rijksmuseum. A late narrative print, possibly a book illustration.
- **Godschalk ondergaat zijn straf en verbrandt zijn boeken** / *Godschalk undergoes his punishment and burns his books* (1807). Graphite; Rijksmuseum via Google Arts and Culture.
- **Verwoesting door de vloed en ijsschotsen te Beusichem, 1809** / *Devastation by flood and ice floes at Beusichem, 1809* (1809). Rijksmuseum via Google Arts and Culture. Late flood print, pairing with W16.
- **Titelpagina voor K. W. Bilderdijk, "De Overstrooming", 1809** / *Title page for Bilderdijk's "The Flood", 1809* (1809). Rijksmuseum via Google Arts and Culture.
- **Gezicht op de haven van Emden** / *View of the port of Emden* (1788-1812). Engraving, height 225 mm x width 265 mm (Artera snippet) -- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED. Rijksmuseum.
- **Reinhart en zijn vrouw met hun baby voor een venster** (before 1792). Engraving, height 238 mm x width 158 mm (Artera snippet) -- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED. Rijksmuseum.
- **Reinhart en oorspronkelijke bewoner van Nederlands-Guyana voeren een gesprek voor diens woning** (1791-1798). Engraving. Rijksmuseum. Colonial conversation piece, pairing with W20.
- **The French eagle in the clamp** (Nov 1813). Draughtsman: anonymous; "Draughtsman: Reinier Vinkeles (I) (rejected attribution)". Measurements h 222 mm x w 184 mm; Rijksmuseum. Included here only as a rejected attribution (leg 2 flag 11) -- do not include it as a Vinkeles work.
- **Prent voor het treurspel "Brutus"** / *Print for the tragedy "Brutus"* (Voltaire). Cited as reference 1 on the Wikipedia page (lib.ugent.be); no date or dimensions established in this leg. The Wikipedia caption pairs it with the Hodges portrait of Vinkeles.
- **NOT his work -- the French eagle in the clamp, November 1813**, is listed above only to prevent the error; it is the one explicitly excluded work in Beyond the 20.

## Evolution

**Phase 1 -- 1760-1765: The coloured view (ages 19-24).** Large watercolour topographical drawings, made ad vivum and in colour: the Schouwburg pendant pair in several versions and sizes (W1, W2, W4). The subject is Amsterdam as a social stage -- crowds arriving and leaving, summer and winter, day and night. Technique is pen, grey/black ink and wash with watercolour over pencil or chalk, on sheets up to about 277 x 434 mm. The audience is the private collector of finished drawings. The driver is already visible: one architecture, varied staffage, light and season as the variables. Van der Willigen's 1817 verdict -- that these coloured theatre drawings are the best of the early work -- has stuck for two centuries because nothing else in the oeuvre is as directly observed.

**Phase 2 -- 1762-1771: Academy and apprenticeship's end (ages 21-30).** Joins the Stadstekenacademie (1762); travels to Brabant with Andriessen and Schmidt (1765, W5); draws his own academy by night (W3, drawn 1764); goes to Paris for a year with Jacques-Philippe Le Bas (1770-71), learning dry-needle etching. The work splits: the coloured drawing continues (W4, 1765), while the engraved line takes over for reproduction. W3 is the hinge -- a drawing made inside the institution, engraved four years later for publication. Scale stays modest (about 35 x 39 cm for the academy etching); the audience shifts from the drawing collector to the print buyer.

**Phase 3 -- 1771-1780: The theatre years and the book trade (ages 30-39).** Back in Amsterdam, Vinkeles works as a printmaker for books: vignettes (W8, 1769, just before this phase), theatre wing sets with his brother Harmanus after other designers (W10-W12, 1775-77), ceremonial entry prints (W9, 1772). The subject is split three ways -- the stage, the city, the book -- and the common technique is the reproductive engraving after another man's design. The workshop is now a family firm ("R. en H. Vinkeles"). The large coloured drawing recedes; the small plate (74 x 113 mm for a vignette) dominates. What he learned drawing crowds now serves the stage set and the ceremony: figures placed to animate architecture, perspective doing the storytelling.

**Phase 4 -- 1780-1795: Portraits, history, atmosphere (ages 39-54).** The portrait factory (W13, 1787, and dozens like it after Buys for dictionaries and histories), Dutch-history prints (Moord op Willem van Oranje, 1782-84), maritime disaster prints, and -- against the grain -- atmospheric city views (W15, fog, 1791-95) and biblical catastrophe (W16, Deluge, 1793). Technique is now predominantly copper engraving and etching, often after Buys or Bulthuis, for publishers such as Jan Allart. The audience is the reading middle class buying illustrated histories and dictionaries. Two opposite tendencies coexist: the standardised, reusable portrait plate, and the expressive small print in which line dissolves into fog or water. The historical portrait and the fog piece are the same hand solving opposite problems -- maximum legibility versus maximum atmosphere.

**Phase 5 -- 1794-1807: The Revolution as a production line (ages 53-66).** With his former pupil Daniel Vrijdag, Vinkeles engraves the Tafereelen van de Staatsomwenteling in Frankrijk -- 25 parts, about 155 plates, issued 1794-1807 (W17, W18, plus the Tuileries, Noyades, States General, Varennes and La Chapelle plates in Beyond the 20). The plates are etchings after French designers (Bulthuis, Monnet, Duplessi-Bertaux, Brion, Vernet, Fragonard fils, Girodet, Swebach and others), lettered with the event date and the engraving date, usually years apart. Scale standardises around 22 x 25-36 cm -- the double-page book plate. The crowd is now a revolutionary crowd, the architecture a fortress or an assembly hall. Vinkeles's name stands second to no one in the lettering, but the design is never his: he is the Amsterdam end of a Franco-Dutch image pipeline, turning French drawings of French events into Dutch books. It is the industrial endpoint of the career that began with a single coloured drawing of a theatre crowd.

**Phase 6 -- 1801-1809: The academy revisited, and the exotic turn (ages 60-68).** The Felix Meritis drawing hall (W19, 1801) returns to the academy interior of W3 at nearly twice the scale (46 x 57.5 cm) -- the room now a purpose-built machine with a wire frame and ropes for posing the model, the cadaver beside the living model. Then the late exotic and disaster prints: Haafner's Ceylon (W20, 1806), the Beusichem flood (1809), Bilderdijk's De Overstrooming title page (1809), the Haremvrouw plates (1803-05). The book illustration never stops, but its geography widens -- Ceylon, Guyana -- and its subjects darken -- flood, punishment, book-burning. He dies in Amsterdam in 1816, aged 74, having left, by the standard count, over 2,500 prints, most of them illustrations to other men's books. The arc is from the unique coloured drawing to the mass-produced plate and back, at the end, to the preparatory drawing (W20) -- the hand returning to paper after forty years of copper.

**What did NOT change.** The medium never changes: engraving (with etching) on paper, from 1760 to 1809. No painting by Vinkeles was found in any source this leg, despite WahooArt listing a "bull and sheep in a landscape" (1765) as a painting -- that attribution is unconfirmed (leg 2, Beyond the 20). Plate sizes stay in two bands throughout -- small (under about 250 mm) for book and portrait work, large (about 300-575 mm) for series and documentary plates -- with the Felix Meritis sheet (46 x 57.5 cm) the largest found. There is no late "free" etched sketch manner in the record; if anything the late work is more documentary (flood, punishment, exotic travel) than the early allegories. And the crowd never leaves: from the Schouwburg steps to the Versailles procession to the Vincennes mob, the same choreography of figures animating architecture runs the whole fifty years.

## Disputes and unverified items (flagged)

- **Birth date: 12 January vs 19 June 1741.** Wikidata-derived infoboxes (EN, NL, DE, RU Wikipedia; Alamy/Wikimedia Commons data) give 12 January 1741. The article bodies of NL/FR/RU Wikipedia, Knoef (1933: "den 19en Juni 1741"), and the scholarly BWN (Jung: "19-06-1741") give 19 June 1741. Even the NL Wikipedia page contradicts itself (infobox 12 January, body 19 June). This study follows BWN (19 June). The 12 January date's origin was not established in this leg (leg 1 F1; see C537).
- **Death date: FR Wikipedia body says 10 June 1816.** FR Wikipedia infobox says 30 January 1816, but its body text says "mort dans cette meme ville le 10 juin 1816". Every other source (BWN, NL/EN/DE/RU Wiki, Knoef) says 30 January 1816. The 10 June date appears to be an error in the FR article (leg 1 F2).
- **Catherine the Great invitation: timing.** Knoef: offers made while in Paris under Le Bas. BWN: offered during the Paris stay by the Russian envoy. NL Wikipedia: "Datzelfde jaar [1771] werd hij uitgenodigd door Catharina de Grote" -- the invitation placed in 1771, the year of his return. Also the post is described variously as "directeur" (NL Wiki) vs "medebestuurder" (BWN, co-director). Minor; the Paris timing (BWN/Knoef) is better sourced than the 1771 dating (leg 1 F3).
- **Felix Meritis hall prints: 1792/1794 vs c. 1800/1801.** Knoef: "Van 1792, resp. '94, gedaan naar de teekeningen van J. Kuiper en P. Barbiers" (the two prints of 1792 and 1794 respectively). BWN: "De mooie, grote platen die hij omstreeks 1801 vervaardigde van de zalen van het gebouw van 'Felix Meritis'". The Met dates its impression "ca. 1800"; RISD dates its impression 1801. Unresolved: either Knoef's dates refer to the drawings, or there are two campaigns, or one source is wrong. Do not assert a single date without noting this (leg 1 F4; see C538).
- **Pupil Jacobus Millies.** Listed in EN Wikipedia (citing RKD) but absent from DE Wikipedia's pupil list and from BWN's (admittedly partial, "onder wie") list. His status as a pupil rests on the RKD/EN-Wiki line only in this leg; the RKD page itself could not be opened (leg 1 F5; see leg 1 F10).
- **"Founder of Pax Artium Nutrix" UNVERIFIED.** The task brief describes Vinkeles as founder of Pax Artium Nutrix. Every source found says only "member" / "lid" (EN Wiki, NL Wiki via RKD). The society met at the Herberg de Zon inn on the Nieuwendijk; Hendrik Kobell also became a member after 1771. No founding role is attested in any source read. Do not call him founder (leg 1 F6; leg 3, section 1, FLAG; see C531).
- **Izaak Schmidt vs Izaak Jansz de Wit.** Two different men are easily blurred: Izaak Schmidt (1740-1818), Brabant travelling companion 1765 and subject of the 1765 triple portrait; and Izaak Jansz de Wit (1744-1809), met in Paris 1770. Some summaries name only one "Izaak". Keep them distinct (leg 1 F7). NOTE: leg 3 could not establish dates for Izaak Schmidt -- the 1740-1818 dates here come from leg 1's narrative, which gives them without a cited source line; treat as search-verified only.
- **Oeuvre count.** "Ca. 2,500 prints" is consistent across Van Eynden and Van der Willigen (1817/1820), Knoef, BWN and the Wikipedias. BWN adds the split: 1,500 after his own drawings. Knoef compares with Le Bas at about 3,000. No contradiction; the count is an estimate, not a catalogue raisonne number (Jung's global inventory is in her unpublished thesis, Bijlage 20, pp. 1-30). Do not present it as 2,500 individual works (leg 1 F8; leg 2 flag 15).
- **"Became a director in 1765" vs "joined 1762".** Consistent across sources once distinguished: member October 1762; seat in the six-man directie after the 1765 reorganisation (BWN, NL Wiki). EN Wiki's bare "became a director" is undated and should not be read as 1762 (leg 1 F9).
- **RKD page not directly readable.** The RKD artist page (RKDartists 81146) could not be opened: searches returned no stable RKD Explore URL, and RKD Explore is JS-driven. RKD-derived facts in this report (birth/death dates 12 January 1741 / 30 January 1816, work locations Amsterdam 1762-1816 / Paris 1770-1771, pupil list) come via the NL Wikipedia citation of RKDartists, not from a direct RKD read. The 12 January birth date in the RKD-derived data conflicts with BWN's 19 June (leg 1 F10).
- **Two museum pages failed to fetch.** (a) British Museum object page for the self-portrait (1867,1214.801) returned only site boilerplate (46 lines, no object data); its facts here (etching + engraving, 101 x 102 mm, purchased from John Deffett Francis, 1867) come from the search-result snippet. (b) The Met's Felix Meritis page (47.100.270) returned HTTP 429 and was not retried; its facts (Vinkeles + Daniel Vrydag, after Pieter Barbiers II, ca. 1800, Harris Brisbane Dick Fund 1947) come from the search-result snippet (leg 1 F11).
- **Key modern sources NOT read end to end.** Jung's unpublished 2005 dissertation and her 2005 Weyerman article ("Groot kunstenaar en eigenzinnige achttiende-eeuwer", pp. 118-133) were not accessible in full text in this leg; Jansen's 1985 letters article was not read end to end -- Vinkeles's own words (Q1-Q8) are known only through BWN's quotations of Jansen. The 1816 obituary "Levensschets van Reinier Vinkeles" (Algemeene Konst- en Letter-bode 11 (1816) 166-169) was not read. Van Eynden and Van der Willigen's Vinkeles entry (vol. II, pp. 316-325) was not read in the original; only its existence, pagination and the 2,500 estimate via secondary citation (leg 1 F12).
- **Drawing Academy etching date range.** V&A: 1764-68. Knoef: the print of a sitting of the academy "uit 1768". Compatible (1768 falls in the range), but the V&A range implies work begun 1764 (leg 1 F13).
- **AI-content and dealer sites repeat unchecked claims.** WahooArt and similar generated pages repeat the Catherine story and the "dry needle" detail without sources; they were not used as evidence. Christie's, Sotheby's and Onno van Seggelen dealer essays were used, but they are commercial texts -- their factual claims (dates, provenance) were cross-checked against BWN/Knoef where possible (leg 1 F14).
- **First child born before the wedding.** BWN: the wedding (ondertrouw 25 May 1781) took place sixteen months after the birth of the first child -- i.e. c. January 1780. Children are counted as 4 sons + 3 daughters born 1780-1794. No contradiction found, but the pre-marital birth is worth noting as a biographical fact, not a scandal claim (leg 1 F15).
- **"Le Bas taught him dry needle etching" vs "dry-needle technique".** Onno van Seggelen says Le Bas "taught the technique of dry needle etching"; BWN says Le Bas was known for his droge-naaldtechniek (dry-needle technique) "waarmee de plaat nog zorgvuldiger kon worden bewerkt". Whether Le Bas's technique was true drypoint (direct needle on copper, with burr) or a related intaglio refinement is not resolved by these sources; the Dutch term is droge naald. Do not over-specify the tool (leg 1 F16).
- **Theatre pendant dating.** Sotheby's: versions dated 1760, 1762 and 1765, with the 1760 example "very finely preserved" and a record price in 2019; a pair dated 1762 in the Amsterdam City Archives; a smaller pair in the John and Marine van Vlissingen Art Foundation. The 1760 date would make the earliest theatre views predate his independence (c. 1762) -- he was still Punt's assistant. Plausible (he was already more collaborator than pupil at 15), but note the tension (leg 1 F17).
- **W3 Drawing Academy -- drawn vs engraved.** V&A dates the print "1764-68"; the plate reads "Delin 1764 et Sculp 1768"; Google Arts and Culture gives "1764-1767" with date created 1768; the Bubb Kuyper impression measures 33.4 x 37.1 cm against the V&A's 34.6 x 39.2 cm. Use "drawn 1764, engraved 1768"; do not quote a single year, and do not treat the two measurements as the same sheet (leg 2 flag 1).
- **W19 Felix Meritis -- 1800 / 1801 / 1802.** RISD dates it 1801; Bubb Kuyper says published by C. S. Roos, Amsterdam, (1802); Meisterdrucke says "ca. 1800". The RISD sheet is 46 x 57.5 cm; the Bubb Kuyper impression is about 42 x 54 cm -- possibly a different impression or a trimmed sheet. Do not merge the two records (leg 2 flag 2).
- **Sotheby's 2024 Keizersgracht text -- recycled wording.** The 2024 page dates the sheet to 1765 but says "twelve years after the present sheet was drawn, the theatre was completely destroyed by fire" (1772) -- twelve years fits a 1760 sheet, not a 1765 one. The sentence is carried over from the 1760 lot description (Sotheby's 2019, lot 105). Treat the 1765 date as the lot's claim and the "twelve years" as a copy error (leg 2 flag 3).
- **Keizersgracht versions -- which sheet is which.** At least five versions exist (1760 night, 1761 day, 1762 pair in the Amsterdam City Archives, 1765 Sotheby's sheet, plus the 1760 Ongpin sheet). Sources disagree on which is the "earliest and largest"; Sotheby's 2019 and Ongpin both claim it for their sheet. Do not call any single sheet the original (leg 2 flag 4).
- **W5 Portraits -- attribution split.** Rijksmuseum/Artera attribute the 1765 group portrait to Vinkeles; Meisterdrucke/Bridgeman list it under Jurriaan Andriessen. The print depicts all three artists; the engraver is disputed (leg 2 flag 5).
- **Tafereelen -- publication date and plate count.** V&A brief descriptions say the series was "published Amsterdam, 1824-25" (a later collected issue); the auction catalogue (RichardMDV / Interencheres, lot 124) dates the serial issue 1794-1807 in 25 parts. V&A calls each plate "one of 81 etchings by the artists"; the auction catalogue counts 155 engraved plates (77 double-page scenes by Vinkeles and Vrijdag plus 78 portraits by Claessens and Porthmann).
