Both numbers are correct for different things (V&A holdings vs full series); do not quote either as the series total without saying which (leg 2 flag 6).
- **Tafereelen lettering -- spelling and typos.** The V&A transcriptions vary: "D. Vrydag" / "D. Vrijdag" / "D. Vrijadg" (Noyades plate, sic), "J. Bukthuis" for Bulthuis, "Monnet, delin." vs "J. Bulthuis, delin." The V&A also spells the artist "Renier Vinkeles" throughout its records, against the standard "Reinier" (leg 2 flag 7).
- **W15 Zware mist -- title date vs making date.** The title says 1790; Artera dates the making to 1791-1795. The 1790 is the depicted fog event (or the subject date), not the engraving date (leg 2 flag 8).
- **Haremvrouw B -- medium confusion.** Artera calls it an engraving dated 1803-1805 and in the same note "a pencil drawn sketch". The two cannot both be the medium; the Rijksmuseum record was not consulted directly. Treat medium as unresolved (leg 2 flag 9).
- **Self-portrait (BM 1867,1214.801) -- no usable date.** Production date is given as the range "1764-1816" (the artist's whole working life). It cannot be placed chronologically and is therefore in Beyond the 20, not in W1-W20 (leg 2 flag 10).
- **The French eagle in the clamp (Nov 1813) -- rejected attribution.** The Rijksmuseum/SuperStock record explicitly marks "Draughtsman: Reinier Vinkeles (I) (rejected attribution)". Do not include it as a Vinkeles work; it is listed in Beyond the 20 only to prevent the error (leg 2 flag 11).
- **Portrait of Ploos van Amstel (1799) -- "possibly by".** Google Arts and Culture / Davison Art Center credit it as "After Cornelis Ploos van Amstel, possibly by Reinier Vinkeles". Do not present it as a certain Vinkeles (leg 2 flag 12).
- **W6 Oost-Indisch Huis -- weak sourcing.** The 1768 date and the image come from the building's Wikipedia article, sourced to Amsterdam Bureau Monumenten and Archeologie. No museum object page for this print was found in this leg. Date and medium should be re-verified against the Rijksmuseum collection before reuse (leg 2 flag 13).
- **Delpher duplicates.** The Delpher results list the same theatre-set titles two to four times (e.g. Armoedige Wooning twice, Inkomst van stadhouder Willem V four times, Het Bosch under both Vinkeles and Groen). These are duplicate index entries / split credits, not separate works. Do not count duplicates as distinct works (leg 2 flag 14).
- **Oeuvre size.** "Over 2,500 prints" (Onno van Seggelen, citing Van Eijnden and Van der Willigen) is the standard figure; it counts prints, mostly book illustrations, and is not a count of distinct designs. Do not present it as 2,500 individual works (leg 2 flag 15).
- **Artera "curatorial notes" are AI-generated and unreliable.** Artera's own pages warn "ASKArtera may not always be accurate. Verify key details." Artera notes were used for nothing in this study beyond title, date, medium, dimensions and location fields, and dimensions taken from Artera are labelled REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED even where the Artera page itself was read end to end. The same class of caution as #542's Artera note.
- **Hermanus Numan birth: 1744 vs 1743.** EN Wikipedia lead gives 1744; the same article cites the KNAW as "Hermanus Numan (1743 - 1820)" (leg 3, section 5).
- **Jan Baptist Xavery death place: Antwerp vs The Hague.** EN Wikipedia and WahooArt give Antwerp; Jeffares pastellists dictionary and a Colnaghi factsheet give The Hague (leg 3, section 5).
- **Louis Fabritius Dubourg spelling.** "Louis Fabricius Dubourg" (EN Wikipedia title) vs "Louis Fabritius Dubourg" (task list, Proantic, Rijksmuseum usage) (leg 3, section 5).
- **Ploos van Amstel birthplace: Weesp vs Amsterdam.** EN Wikipedia and Jeffares give Weesp; giftpals.com says "born in Amsterdam" -- a low-quality source, treat as source error (leg 3, section 5).
- **"C. Bagerts" = Cornelis Bogerts.** Spelling variant, same man (EN Wikipedia Pieter Barbiers vs AbeBooks Utrecht print) (leg 3, section 5).
- **Jakob van der Schley death: 7 vs 12 February 1779.** EN Wikipedia gives 7 February 1779; Geographicus gives 12 February 1779 (re-confirmed from #542).
- **Bernard Picart Amsterdam move: 1710 vs 1711.** Encyclopedia.com and McClintock and Strong give 1710; EN Wikipedia and GAMEO give 1711 (re-confirmed from #542).
- **Jacobus Houbraken Amsterdam move: 1707 vs 1709.** EN Wikipedia, NPG and Britannica give 1707; geerts.com gives 1709 (re-confirmed from #542; #542's flag said 1710 per the British Museum -- the 1709 variant is from geerts.com, noted here).
- **Jan de Beijer death place: Emmerich vs Doesburg vs Kleve.** EN Wikipedia Jan de Beijer gives "near Kleve" / Emmerich, 15 February 1780, vs Doesburg also mentioned (re-confirmed from #542).
- **Albert Xavery (father of Jan Baptist): 1664-1724 vs 1664-1728.** Sotheby's gives 1664-1724; Colnaghi factsheet gives 1664-1728 (re-confirmed from #542).
- **Jan Caspar Philips dates.** Lead gives (1690-1775) but a portrait caption in the same article says "(1700-65)"; one source gives (1695-1773) (carried from #542).
- **Jacob van Huysum birth.** Bapt. 25 February 1688 (Wikipedia) vs 1687 vs c.1687 vs 1686 (carried from #542).
- **Ploos "1559-1760" typo for 1759-1760** (in some source; not seen this leg) (carried from #542).
- **Barbiers generations conflated in Delpher.** Coulisse designs dated 1794, 1788 and 1787 belong to later generations (Pieter II/III), not Pieter I (carried from #542).
- **Pothoven was NOT Punt's pupil.** Corrected in #542 before proposing; carried here so the error is not reintroduced (carried from #542).
- **Bloemen and Marcus have no dates established (propose only if a later leg finds dates).** Marcus's dates (1774-1826) were found by leg 3 of this run, so he is proposed as a main in "New from #543" (see C533); Bloemen's dates are still not established (carried from #542).
- **Jan de Groot conflation.** Getty ULAN says de Wit was "student of Jan de Groot (1650-1726)" vs the RKD teacher list; the Hague pupil is Johannes de Groot (carried from #542).

## Dimensions verified vs not verified (tally)

- VERIFIED (museum, university-museum or dealer page read end to end): **4 in the 20** -- W3, W17, W18, W19 -- **plus 2 in Beyond the 20** (Tuileries, Noyades), for 6 verified dimensions in total.
- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED (dimensions from search snippets or dealer/AI-mirror pages only): **7** -- W1, W2, W4, W5, W7, W8, W15.
- NOT VERIFIED (no dimensions found anywhere): **9** -- W6, W9, W10, W11, W12, W13, W14, W16, W20.
- Total: 4 + 7 + 9 = 20.

## Aesthetic logic

Vinkeles's aesthetic is the logic of the working engraver, not the logic of the original. Everything in his system serves one question: how does a hand that can do anything decide what to do, and how does it keep doing it for fifty years? The burin answers first: tone is line weight and spacing, velvety black to pure white, and the plate needs no colour at all. The dry needle answers second: Paris taught him a softer line, and the same hand can now hold the crisp burin and the tender drypoint in one image. The portrait answers third: the frame eats the portrait decade by decade, ornament draining out until only a bust in a plain rectangle remains, because the engraver's job is to choose the register for the sitter, not to decorate him. The city view answers fourth: the crowd is the subject and the architecture is the stage -- tiny figures do the looking for us, and the same facade re-drawn as a day/night, summer/winter pair turns a view into a system. The theatre answers fifth: the light source is the plot, daylight through tall windows against a single candle in darkness, and the figures are placed where the light falls. The vignette answers sixth: a whole landscape with waterfall and monument compressed into a two-inch tailpiece, because the book page is the frame and smallness is the skill. And the career answers last: the three-period arc is the system made visible -- the early hand mixing Punt's classicism with Fokke's lively manner, the middle hand at its minute and subtle best, the late hand loosening into hasty, rough etchings as the letters grow gloomier and the wife dies and the work becomes a means of survival. What holds it all together is the academy: the room with the model on the platform and the students in a ring, drawn by night in 1764 and engraved again as a purpose-built machine in 1801, with a wire frame and ropes and a flayed cadaver beside the living body. The school is the print's afterlife -- the book teaches the hand, the hand teaches the room, and the room, lit by one lamp, is the picture Vinkeles kept returning to for forty years. His palette logic follows: there is no palette, only density -- and his tragedy is that the public paid him to fritter his time on little book prints while the big plates, the Felix Meritis halls, the high point of the oeuvre, could not feed seven children.

## Positions

**P1 -- No North-Netherlandish engraver could equal him.** No North-Netherlandish engraver could equal the quality of Vinkeles's prints, let alone the enormous size of his oeuvre. -- Carlien Jung, BWN closing judgment (BWN L318-L324). ABOUT.

**P2 -- A great artist and a headstrong eighteenth-century man.** He was a "Groot kunstenaar en eigenzinnige achttiende-eeuwer" -- a great artist and a headstrong/eccentric eighteenth-century man. -- Carlien Jung, title of her 2005 article, Mededelingen van de Stichting Jacob Campo Weyerman 28 (2005) 118-133. ABOUT.

**P3 -- Engraving is not mere copying.** Engraving is not mere copying: the interpreter's personality plays a meaningful role in the transposition, and the engraver's figure can be a moment of high importance in that transfer. -- J. Knoef, 1933, opening argument (Knoef L13-L17). ABOUT (general claim, Vinkeles as the case).

**P4 -- Until 1790 his best work belonged to the best in the field.** Until 1790 his carefully portrayed prints belonged to the best in his field; his enormous productivity sometimes led to superficiality, but his best work -- above all his early work -- is highly valued. -- Dutch Wikipedia, summarising the literature (NL Wiki L46-L52). ABOUT.

**P5 -- More modest than the great Frenchmen, but not to be dismissed.** Set against the great Frenchmen of the period -- Moreau, Cochin, Eisen -- Vinkeles is of more modest stature; but it would be equally unjust to close one's eyes to the many excellent things that came from his hand and to reject him with his contemporaries because they did not realise the highest attainable. -- J. Knoef (Knoef L120-L123). ABOUT.

**P6 -- He overestimated his powers in history painting.** In history painting and sacred history he overestimated his powers and entirely lacked the qualities that must count as the first condition for those fields: living through the material and the ability to make it speak in its greatness. The illustration for Hamelsveld's Kerkelijke Geschiedenis is the eloquent proof. -- J. Knoef (Knoef L124-L131). ABOUT.

**P7 -- The pre-romantic subjects are the convincing ones.** More convincing are the pre-romantic subjects: people of his own time, in wild storm nights or on clear moonlit nights, turning inward after their playful pleasures, musing sadly or tenderly by a ruined cloister or in a churchyard, or seeing ghost apparitions -- because here time itself supplies the players, the decor and the sentiment, so the gap between reality and fantasy is far smaller than in the history piece. -- J. Knoef (Knoef L135-L140). ABOUT.

**P8 -- The coloured theatre drawings stand out.** "Among his early drawings, those in color depicting the entering and exiting of the Amsterdam Theater stand out." -- Adriaan van der Willigen, 1817, quoted in Sotheby's lot essay (Sotheby's L2-L3). ABOUT. (Van der Willigen is the near-contemporary biographer; the remark is from 1817, the year after Vinkeles's death.)

**P9 -- The Felix Meritis halls are the high point.** The Felix Meritis hall engravings are the high point (hoogtepunt) of his oeuvre -- but he could not live from such work. -- BWN/Jung (BWN L250-L254) and Knoef (Knoef L194-L198, on the two prints as a monument of contemporary social life). ABOUT.

**P10 -- He quickly fell into oblivion.** Despite the many words of praise after his death, he quickly fell into oblivion. -- BWN/Jung (BWN L316-L318). ABOUT.

## Quotes

Q1-Q8 are BY Vinkeles, all via his letters to Volkert van der Plaats, transcribed in Guido Jansen, Jaarboek Amstelodamum 27 (1985) 107-121, as quoted in BWN. Dutch originals given; translations are leg 1's. Q9-Q21 are ABOUT him. Q22 is BY Jacob Haafner, from his circle -- context for the Haafner plates, not Vinkeles's own view (see C535).

- **Q1** (BY, letter to Volkert van der Plaats, 19 April 1800): "Man Lief onze kinderen beginnen ons te doen denken [dat] wij onze jaren krijgen" -- "My dear, our children are beginning to make us think [that] we are getting on in years."
- **Q2** (BY, same letter, 19 April 1800): "Oud worden foeij dat is leelijk" -- "Growing old, fie, that is ugly."
- **Q3** (BY, 1800 letter): "Ik voor mij doe alles noch met hetzelfde gemak als voor 25 jaaren" -- "For my part I still do everything with the same ease as 25 years ago."
- **Q4** (BY, 1800): "minder lust" in het leven te hebben, omdat "de Geestelijken Boosheid in de lugt los gebroken is" -- having "less desire" for life, because "the Clerical Wickedness has broken loose in the air."
- **Q5** (BY, on the death of his wife Catharina, 21 April 1802, to Van der Plaats): "Wat een gemis voor mij" -- "What a loss for me."
- **Q6** (BY, 21 April 1802): "Voortaan zal mijne woning eene spelonke der droefheid wesen. Het licht dat warmte en genoegen versprijde is uitgedoofd. Ruim drie en twintig Jaaren ben ik eene der gelukkigste stervelingen geweest, helaas geweest" -- "Henceforth my home shall be a den of sorrow. The light that spread warmth and pleasure is extinguished. For well over twenty-three years I have been one of the happiest mortals -- alas, have been."
- **Q7** (BY, letter to Van der Plaats, 16 September 1801, on the criticism of his work): "de Posteriteyt zal kunnen oordeelen" -- "posterity will be able to judge."
- **Q8** (BY, 16 September 1801): "ik iets meer als boekprentjes hebbe kunne maken, en dat het niet mijn maar wel het GeEerde Publicq haar schuld is [dat] ik mijn tijd met de laatste hebbe moeten verbeuzelen" -- "I could have made something more than little book prints, and it is not my fault but that of the honoured Public that I had to fritter away my time on the latter."
- **Q9** (ABOUT, Adriaan van der Willigen, 1817, quoted in Sotheby's lot essay for The Keizersgracht, Amsterdam, with the entrance to the Schouwburg): "Among his early drawings, those in color depicting the entering and exiting of the Amsterdam Theater stand out."
- **Q10** (ABOUT, J. Knoef, Elseviers Geillustreerd Maandschrift 43 (1933), opening of the biographical section): "Het is het werk van een in zijn tijd vermaard graveur der 18e eeuw, van Reinier Vinkeles, dat hier ingeleid moge worden om den lezer te doen zien hoeveel schoons ook de gravure kan bieden." -- "It is the work of a famous engraver of the 18th century in his time, Reinier Vinkeles, that may be introduced here to show the reader how much beauty engraving can offer."
- **Q11** (ABOUT, J. Knoef on engraving in general, applied to Vinkeles): "Een rijke verscheidenheid van tinten tusschen fluweelig zwart en het pure wit van het papier, een virtuoze hanteering van de lijn, van haar-dunne tot forsch-gekorvene" -- "A rich variety of tints between velvety black and the pure white of the paper, a virtuoso handling of the line, from hair-thin to boldly incised."
- **Q12** (ABOUT, J. Knoef, comparing Vinkeles and Van der Meer as interpreters of Jacobus Buys): "Blijkt de eerste forscher en krachtiger, Vinkeles is ongetwijfeld fijner en subtieler in zijn effecten." -- "If the first [Van der Meer] proves bolder and more powerful, Vinkeles is undoubtedly finer and subtler in his effects."
- **Q13** (ABOUT, J. Knoef): "Tegenover figuren als Moreau, Cochin, Eisen is Vinkeles van bescheidener statuur" -- "Set against figures such as Moreau, Cochin, Eisen, Vinkeles is of more modest stature."
- **Q14** (ABOUT, Carlien Jung, BWN): "geen Noord-Nederlandse graveur de kwaliteit van zijn prenten kon of kan evenaren en zeker de enorme omvang van zijn oeuvre niet" -- "no North-Netherlandish engraver could or can equal the quality of his prints, and certainly not the enormous size of his oeuvre."
- **Q15** (ABOUT, quoted by Knoef, about 18th-century art in general, applied to Vinkeles's history pieces; Edmond and Jules de Goncourt, L'Art du dix-huitieme siecle, vol. 11, p. 13): "Pauvre art du haut en bas et des grands aux petits, l'art du XVIIIe siecle, lorsqu'il se depayse, lorsqu'il sort de la representation du temps, lorsqu'il va aux grandeurs, aux poesies, aux majestes, aux terreurs du passe, de l'histoire, ou du genie" -- "Poor art from top to bottom and from the great to the small, the art of the 18th century, when it displaces itself, when it leaves the representation of its own time, when it goes to the grandeurs, the poetries, the majesties, the terrors of the past, of history, or of genius."
- **Q16** (ABOUT, Carlien Jung, BWN, on the school anecdote): "Of dit verhaal op waarheid berust, is onzeker." -- "Whether this story is true is uncertain."
- **Q17** (ABOUT, Dutch Wikipedia): "Zijn enorme productiviteit -- Vinkeles' oeuvre wordt geschat op ca. 2.500 prenten -- leidde soms tot oppervlakkigheid, maar zijn beste werk (vooral zijn vroegere) wordt hogelijk gewaardeerd." -- "His enormous productivity -- Vinkeles's oeuvre is estimated at c. 2,500 prints -- sometimes led to superficiality, but his best work (above all his earlier work) is highly valued."
- **Q18** (ABOUT, Christie's lot essay, A view of the Oude Kerk, Amsterdam): "Part of the charm of Vinkeles' townscapes lies in the staffage; here, two men are looking at the Oude Kerk (which still stands today) in admiration."
- **Q19** (ABOUT, J. Knoef on Vinkeles's vignettes): "Gracieuze niemendalletjes, de attributen der poezie, luchtig met linten omstrikt, wat korenaren en een bloem" -- "Graceful trifles, the attributes of poetry, lightly bound with ribbons, some ears of corn and a flower."
- **Q20** (ABOUT, J. Knoef on the later portraits): "Eenvoud, natuurlijkheid, doch in een andere nuance, zouden nog in toenemende mate het wachtwoord van komende jaren worden." -- "Simplicity, naturalness, but in a different nuance, would increasingly become the watchword of the coming years."
- **Q21** (ABOUT, Carlien Jung, BWN): "Toch raakte hij -- ondanks de vele lovende woorden na zijn overlijden -- snel in de vergetelheid." -- "Yet, despite the many words of praise after his death, he quickly fell into oblivion."
- **Q22** (CONTEXT, BY Jacob Haafner, not by Vinkeles; from his circle -- Haafner was briefly apprenticed in Vinkeles's workshop): "The discovery of India by the Europeans is the biggest disaster this country has been able to face." Jacob Haafner, quoted on the Onno van Seggelen page about the two Vinkeles drawings for Haafner's Lotgevallen (1806). Included as context for the Haafner plates, not as Vinkeles's own view; Haafner is described there as "one of the first europeans to revolt against colonialism".

## Network

Teachers: 1. Jan Punt (1711-1779) -- Vinkeles studied with him "for some ten years" from about age eleven and was his assistant for over a decade. (EN Wikipedia, Reinier Vinkeles, read end to end, 39 lines; Onno van Seggelen dealer page.) Punt is study #542. 2. Jacques-Philippe Le Bas (1707-1783) -- Vinkeles studied a year with him in Paris in 1770-71 and learned dry-needle etching from him. (EN Wikipedia Vinkeles; EN Wikipedia Le Bas confirms 8 July 1707 - 14 April 1783, head of the largest engraving workshop in Paris.) Proposed as main 4 for #544.

Travelling companions: 3. Jurriaan Andriessen (1742-1819) -- travelled with Vinkeles to Brabant in 1765. (EN Wikipedia Vinkeles; Alamy/WGA captions give 1742-1819.) Vinkeles's 1765 triple portrait shows Schmidt, Andriessen and himself (Google Arts and Culture). Proposed as main 2 for #544. 4. Izaak Schmidt -- the other Brabant companion, 1765; also in the 1765 triple portrait. No dates established -- no source found gives his birth/death years. FLAG. NOTE: leg 1's narrative gives Schmidt's dates as 1740-1818 without a cited source line; leg 3 could not establish them -- treat as search-verified only, not settled.

People met in Paris: 5. Hermanus Numan (1744 - 9 March 1820) -- met in Paris 1770. Numan himself travelled to Paris in 1768, studied engraving there, then studied under Andriessen at the Stadstekenacademie in 1771 and later joined Felix Meritis. (EN Wikipedia Vinkeles; EN Wikipedia Hermanus Numan.) Proposed as main 3 for #544. FLAG: the same Numan article cites the KNAW as "Hermanus Numan (1743 - 1820)" -- birth year 1744 vs 1743. 6. Izaak Jansz de Wit (1744-1809) -- met in Paris 1770. (EN Wikipedia Vinkeles.) Proposed as reserve for #544. NOTE: a different man from Jacob de Wit, study #541 -- near-name collision, do not conflate.

Academy colleagues and Pax Artium Nutrix circle: 7. Hendrik Kobell (1751-1779) -- fellow member of Pax Artium Nutrix; at the club he "became friendly with prominent collectors such as Jacob de Vos and Cornelis Ploos van Amstel". (Web Gallery of Art biography of Hendrik Kobell.) Proposed as alternate 1 for #544. NOTE: a different man from Wilhelm von Kobell, study #540. 8. Jacob de Vos -- collector, Pax circle via Kobell (WGA, as above). No dates established. 9. Cornelis Ploos van Amstel (1726-1798) -- Pax circle via Kobell; and direct collaborator: "Together with Reinier Vinkeles, Jacob Houbraken, Jan de Beijer, and Caspar Jacobsz Philips, he illustrated a new atlas of Amsterdam." (EN Wikipedia Ploos, read end to end, 49 lines.) Carried #542 main 4; now the #544 top pick's circle, not re-proposed here. 10. Anna de Jong -- maid at Herberg de Zon on the Nieuwendijk, where "the meetings of artists' association Pax Artium Nutrix, 'Peace feeds art', took place"; Vinkeles etched her portrait. (Rijksmuseum/Alamy object records.) No dates. 11. H.O. Arntzenius (1743-1795) -- "lid van het kunstminnend gezelschap Pax Artium Nutrix"; his portrait is by Vinkeles. (Amsterdam City Archives via Alamy.)

FLAG -- "founder" unverified. The brief calls Vinkeles a founder of Pax Artium Nutrix. Every source read says only that he "was a member of the artist's club Pax Artium Nutrix" (EN Wikipedia Vinkeles). Two targeted searches found no founder list and no founding date for the club. Do not carry "founder" into the study without a source.

Pupils (6, all named in EN Wikipedia Vinkeles): 12. Jacob Ernst Marcus (1774-1826) -- "one of the few pupils Vinkeles accepted"; also trained in drawing by Steven Goble; gold medal at the Stadstekenacademie 1798; later professor/director of the Akademie van Beeldende Kunsten (1820); founder of the society Kunst Zij Ons Doel with colleagues Harmanus Fock and Harmanus Vinkeles. (Stephen Ongpin; Leiden Special Collections blog; Jeffares.) His 1812 etchings portray Jacobus Buys, Izaak Schmidt and Jurriaan Andriessen -- the Vinkeles circle, one generation on. Proposed as main 5 for #544. 13. Jacobus Millies -- named as a pupil and nothing more. No dates, no works found -- a dedicated search returned only the Vinkeles article itself. FLAG: propose only if a later leg finds dates/works. 14. Abraham Vinkeles -- his son. No dates established. 15. Harmanus Vinkeles (1745-1804) -- his brother. (EN Wikipedia Vinkeles.) 16. Johannes Vinkeles -- his son. No dates established. 17. Daniel Vrijdag (1765-1822) -- pupil; later co-engraver with Vinkeles of the 77 double-page scenes in Tafereelen van de Staatsomwenteling in Frankrijk (Amsterdam, Jan Allart, 1794-1807), and co-engraver of the Felix Meritis Drawing Academy print (Met 47.100.270, ca. 1800).

Workshop: 18. Jacob Haafner (1754-1809) -- travel writer; "In 1770, he returned to Amsterdam and became an apprentice to painter and engraver Reinier Vinkeles" (EN Wikipedia Jacob Haafner); introduced to Vinkeles by the merchant J. Hilkes and "got an apprenticeship at Vinkeles' workshop for some time" (Onno van Seggelen). Vinkeles later made the drawings for Haafner's Ceylon/India plates. Not an artist by trade -- a writer who passed through the workshop. Proposed as spare 2 for #544. 19. J. Hilkes -- merchant who introduced Haafner to Vinkeles. No dates.

Publishers / book trade: 20. Jan (Johannes) Allart -- Amsterdam publisher of the Tafereelen (1794-1807) and of the Protestant-portrait series Vinkeles engraved (Delpher lists "Amsterdam: Johannes Allart"). Jan = Johannes; count once. 21. Abraham Blusse -- Dordrecht publisher of Weyerman's Levens-Beschryvingen vol. 4 (1769); Vinkeles's vignette in it is after Daniel Kerkhoff. (British Museum object P_1874-0613-2437.) 22. C.S. Roos -- Amsterdam publisher of an 1802 engraving after P. Barbiers and J. Kuyper. (Invaluable listing.) 23. Pierre Fouquet Jr. (1729-1800) -- publisher/art dealer of the Nieuwe atlas of Amsterdam (1760-1805 editions), the atlas Vinkeles co-illustrated with Ploos, Houbraken, de Beijer and Caspar Philips. (AbeBooks/dealer listing for the Fouquet atlas.)

Collaborators -- designers whose work Vinkeles engraved: 24. Jacobus Buys (1724-1801) -- Vinkeles engraved many portraits "naar J. Buys" (after Buys), including the 1787 Petrus Bertius and the Chalmot Biographisch woordenboek series (J. Allart, 1798-1800). (Van Someren catalogue via archive.org; Invaluable.) Carried #541 main. 25. Pieter Barbiers II (1749-1842) -- the Felix Meritis Drawing Academy print is "After Pieter Barbiers II" and "After Jacques Kuyper", engraved by Vinkeles and Vrijdag. (Met; RISD.) Proposed as spare 1 for #544. NOTE: son of Pieter Barbiers I (carried #542 spare); do not conflate generations. 26. Jacques Kuyper (1761-1808) -- same print, after Kuyper. (Met; RISD.) Proposed as alternate 2 for #544. 27. Cornelis Bogerts (1745-1817) -- joint "Historical print of Utrecht" by Vinkeles and Bogerts (1790); Wikipedia's Barbiers article spells him "C. Bagerts", who engraved Barbiers's 1772 theatre-fire picture -- spelling variant, same man. (AbeBooks; EN Wikipedia Pieter Barbiers.) Carried #542 extra verified spare. 28. Daniel Kerkhoff (?) -- Vinkeles vignette after Kerkhoff (BM). The "(?)" is the BM's own; FLAG as uncertain attribution.

Portraitist of Vinkeles; co-teacher; second-order: 29. Charles Howard Hodges -- painted the portrait of Vinkeles used as the lead image on his Wikipedia article. No dates established this leg. 30. Steven Goble -- Marcus's drawing teacher alongside Vinkeles (co-teacher, not Vinkeles's own teacher). No dates established. 31. Harmanus Fock -- Marcus's colleague in founding Kunst Zij Ons Doel (with Harmanus Vinkeles). Second-order link only; no dates established. FLAG.

Living portrait sitters: 32. Gerrit Braamcamp (1699-1771) -- Amsterdam merchant and collector; Vinkeles's 1766 engraving shows him holding a female bust (Rijksmuseum; Invaluable notes the print is after Jacob Savery). 33. Albertus van Rijssel (1726-1805) -- major general; Vinkeles copper engraving, three-quarter bust with cannon and cannonballs. (Invaluable.) 34. Engelbert Francois van Berckel -- portrait 1787. (Google Arts and Culture.)

NOT counted in the 34 (listed separately, no personal contact): Posthumous portrait subjects (engraved after Buys or from older images, all long dead): Petrus Bertius (d. 1629), Franciscus Gomarus, Caspar Coolhaes, Festus Hommius, Menno Simons, Petrus Plancius, Ulrich Zwingli (Delpher Protestant-portraits series, 1788-1796). French source artists for the Tafereelen (Vinkeles/Vrijdag engraved after their designs; no evidence of personal contact): Brion, Duplessi-Bertaux, Ozanne, Vernet, Fragonard fils, Girodet, Swebach. The Tafereelen portraits (78) were by Claessens and Porthmann, not by Vinkeles/Vrijdag. Jacob Savery -- Braamcamp portrait is after him (d. 1603); a source, not a contact.

Cross-links worth noting: Vinkeles to Punt to de Wit to Rubens -- the chain this study continues: Punt engraved de Wit's copies of Rubens's ceilings; Vinkeles studied ten years with Punt and then taught from the Teekenboek at the academy. Vinkeles to Buys to Ploos to Troost -- Buys designed the portraits Vinkeles engraved; Buys taught Ploos; Ploos married Elisabeth Troost, daughter of Cornelis Troost (carried #541 main). Ploos + Vinkeles + Houbraken + Jan de Beijer + Caspar Jacobsz Philips illustrated the new atlas of Amsterdam; Ploos + Schley catalogued Amsterdam City Hall art. Marcus to Buys to Schmidt to Andriessen -- Vinkeles's best-documented pupil etched the portraits of the master's own circle a generation on (Buys, Schmidt, Andriessen, 1812). Numan to Andriessen to Vinkeles -- met in Paris 1770, then Numan studied under Andriessen at the Stadstekenacademie in 1771. Haafner to Vinkeles to Ceylon -- the workshop apprentice became the travel writer whose book Vinkeles illustrated at 65.

Influence line: Vinkeles's documented influence line runs through the academy and the workshop, not through a single mechanism. His pupils (Marcus, Millies, Abraham and Johannes Vinkeles, Harmanus Vinkeles, Vrijdag) extend the line one generation further, and Marcus's handling derives from Vinkeles's late, looser manner (Knoef) -- the only named pupil whose style is traced to the master's late work. The documented influence is the institution itself: the Stadstekenacademie, directed by Vinkeles for 43 years, teaching from books like the Teekenboek; the Felix Meritis drawing hall, engraved by Vinkeles and Vrijdag as a purpose-built machine for looking; and the Amsterdam atlas, the collective work of Vinkeles, Houbraken, Ploos, de Beijer and Caspar Philips engraving the city together. No named pupil of Vinkeles is found carrying a particular Vinkeles mechanism forward -- the line continues as a school, not as a style.

## Future candidates
Top pick for #544 is Bernard Picart (11 June 1673 - 8 May 1733) - the trunk of the whole Amsterdam engraving line Vinkeles inherited. French draughtsman, engraver and book illustrator working in Amsterdam; son of Etienne Picart, called le Romain; taught by Charles Le Brun; received into the Academie royale in 1689; Antwerp 1696; settled in Amsterdam 1711. His Ceremonies et coutumes religieuses de tous les peuples du monde (1723-1743, with the publisher Jean Frederic Bernard; 266 engravings, 10 volumes) put the world's religions into print; also the Temple of the Muses (1733) and Impostures innocentes (1734). He ran an engraving school: pupils included Jacob Folkema, Jakob van der Schley, Pieter Tanje, Francois Morellon la Cave and Johann Philipp Endelich. His widow's inventory of 12 March 1736 listed about 400 portfolios of copperplates and 54 paintings; the Rijksmuseum holds 2,000+ of his works. He is the top pick because he is the trunk of the Amsterdam engraving line: he taught Folkema, Schley and Tanje, ran an engraving school in Amsterdam, collaborated with Houbraken and Gerard Hoet on the great picture bible, and with Jean-Frederic Bernard produced the Ceremonies -- the first comparative, quasi-ethnographic survey of world religions, drawn from travellers' accounts he never saw first-hand. Studying him first unlocks the next three queued names at once (Folkema and Schley are his pupils, Dubourg his 1729 collaborator), and his mechanism -- the book as a visual encyclopaedia, assembled from second-hand reports into a single consistent engraved system -- is the direct ancestor of Vinkeles's own trade: book illustration, portrait series, and the Amsterdam atlas. A study of Picart carries the chain from the Frenchman who taught Amsterdam to engrave to the Amsterdam engravers who taught Vinkeles's master, and from the encyclopaedia to the book illustration. The doorway is the French engraver who taught Tanje his trade and put the world's religions into print from Amsterdam. Jurriaan Andriessen (1742-1819; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Brabant companion who painted the walls of Amsterdam houses and taught the next engraver in the chain. Wall-covering and theatre-decoration painter; travelled to Brabant with Vinkeles and Izaak Schmidt in 1765; subject, with Schmidt and Vinkeles himself, of the 1765 triple portrait; teacher of Hermanus Numan at the Stadstekenacademie in 1771. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the travelling companion, not the trunk: the painter beside the engraver on the road, whose wall-coverings are the large-scale cousin of Vinkeles's theatre sets. The doorway is the Brabant companion who painted the walls of Amsterdam houses -- and taught the next engraver in the chain. Hermanus Numan (1744-1820; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Paris meeting who became the copyist, theorist and publisher of the Amsterdam engraving line. Engraver, copyist, theorist and publisher; met Vinkeles in Paris in 1770; travelled to Paris in 1768, studied engraving there, then studied under Andriessen at the Stadstekenacademie in 1771; later Felix Meritis like Vinkeles and Vrijdag. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the theorist of the line, not its trunk: the man who wrote about engraving as well as engraving, and whose career runs parallel to Vinkeles's from the same Paris meeting. The doorway is the Paris meeting who became the copyist, theorist and publisher of the Amsterdam engraving line. Jacques-Philippe Le Bas (1707-1783; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Paris master whose dry needle Vinkeles crossed Europe to learn, and whose workshop was the largest in Paris. French engraver; head of the largest engraving workshop in Paris; teacher of Cochin, Cathelin, Strange and Ryland, as well as of Vinkeles for a year in 1770-71; Vinkeles lived in his house and learned his dry-needle technique there. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the French master behind the Dutch pupil: the technique itself, not the Amsterdam line -- and a study of him is a study of Paris, not of the chain that runs through Punt. The doorway is the Paris master whose dry needle Vinkeles crossed Europe to learn -- and whose workshop was the largest in Paris. Jacob Ernst Marcus (1774-1826; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the pupil who took Vinkeles's late, loose manner and turned it into a school of his own. Engraver; "one of the few pupils Vinkeles accepted"; also trained in drawing by Steven Goble; gold medal at the Stadstekenacademie 1798; later professor/director of the Akademie van Beeldende Kunsten (1820); founder of the society Kunst Zij Ons Doel with Harmanus Fock and Harmanus Vinkeles; his 106-print Studiebeelden en Fragmenten (1807-16) is the next generation's drawing book; his 1812 etchings portray Jacobus Buys, Izaak Schmidt and Jurriaan Andriessen -- the Vinkeles circle, one generation on. He is main 5 rather than top pick because he is the continuation after Vinkeles, not the trunk before him: the pupil whose handling derives from Vinkeles's late manner, and the proof the school outlived the master. The doorway is the pupil who took Vinkeles's late, loose manner and turned it into a school of his own. (The 15 names carried in #542 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Bernard Picart (11 June 1673 - 8 May 1733), Jacobus Houbraken (25 December 1698 - 14 November 1780), Cornelis Ploos van Amstel (4 January 1726 - 20 December 1798) (mains); Louis Fabritius Dubourg (bapt. 5 July 1693 - buried 22 September 1775) (reserve); Simon Fokke (1712-1784), Jakob van der Schley (26 July 1715 - 7 February 1779) (alternates); spares: Jan Caspar Philips (1690-1775), Pieter Barbiers I (bapt. 23 April 1717 - 7 September 1780), Jan Baptist Xavery (Antwerp 1697 - 1742, The Hague), Gerard Joseph Xavery (27 July 1700 - after 1747), Jacob Folkema (1692-1767), Jan de Beijer (24 September 1703 - c.1780), Jacob van Huysum (bapt. 25 February 1688 - 1740), Caspar Jacobsz Philips (1732-1789). Bernard Picart (11 June 1673 - 8 May 1733) - the French engraver who taught Tanje his trade and put the world's religions into print from Amsterdam. French draughtsman, engraver and book illustrator working in Amsterdam; son of Etienne Picart, called le Romain; taught by Charles Le Brun; received into the Academie royale in 1689; Antwerp 1696; settled in Amsterdam 1711. His Ceremonies et coutumes religieuses de tous les peuples du monde (1723-1743, with the publisher Jean Frederic Bernard; 266 engravings, 10 volumes) put the world's religions into print; also the Temple of the Muses (1733, published by Zacharias Chatelain) and Impostures innocentes (1734, published by his widow). He ran an engraving school: pupils included Jacob Folkema, Jakob van der Schley, Pieter Tanje, Francois Morellon la Cave and Johann Philipp Endelich. His widow's inventory of 12 March 1736 listed about 400 portfolios of copperplates and 54 paintings; the Rijksmuseum holds 2,000+ of his works. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the master before Punt, not the continuation after him: the Frenchman whose school taught the Amsterdam engravers Punt worked beside, and the man who turned engraving into an encyclopaedia. The doorway is the French engraver who taught Tanje his trade and put the world's religions into print from Amsterdam. Jacobus Houbraken (25 December 1698 - 14 November 1780) - the son who engraved his father's book and then engraved four hundred faces, many of them the only ones left. Son of Arnold Houbraken (carried #535 spare); moved to Amsterdam 1707; helped his father with the Groote Schouburgh (1718-21); made 400+ portrait engravings, often the only surviving likenesses of the sitters; Heads of Illustrious Persons of Great Britain (1743-52, with Thomas Birch and George Vertue); engraved for Wagenaar's Vaderlandsche historie 1752-59 for Isaac Tirion; portraits for van Gool's Nieuwe Schouburg (1750-51); in his 80th year engraved scenes from Troost's De Ontdekte Schijndeugd; his models were Cornelis Cort, Jonas Suyderhoef, Gerard Edelinck and the Visscher family; also a dealer and collector of Rembrandt etchings. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the portrait engraver, not the academy man: the face as a record, where Vinkeles is the school as a record. The doorway is the son who engraved his father's book and then engraved four hundred faces, many of them the only ones left. Jacobus is proposable; Arnold stays carried and is not re-proposed. Cornelis Ploos van Amstel (4 January 1726 - 20 December 1798) - the lumber merchant who married Troost's daughter, collected five thousand drawings, and invented a way to print them a century before photography. Pupil of Norbert van Bloemen and of Jacobus Buys (carried #541 main); married Elisabeth Troost, daughter of Cornelis Troost (carried #541 main); a lumber merchant by trade and a collector of 5,000+ drawings including Rembrandts. He invented the prenttekening -- a transfer/offset method of reproducing drawings in the crayon manner, from 1765; his Hoofdwerk ran to 46 prints at 350 impressions each. His collection sold in 1800 for 109,406 guilders. His pupils included Christina Chalon, William V Prince of Orange, Elisabeth van Woensel and Hermanus Petrus Schouten; he reported Petrus Camper's facial-angle findings; a member of Arte et Amicitia. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the collector's answer to the engraver's question: not the man who made the prints, but the man who figured out how to print the drawings themselves, and the proof that the print trade had a second half Punt never touched. The doorway is the lumber merchant who married Troost's daughter, collected five thousand drawings, and invented a way to print them a century before photography. Louis Fabritius Dubourg (bapt. 5 July 1693 - buried 22 September 1775) - the history painter who swapped the brush for the burin and taught the academy circle to etch. History painter who became an engraver; pupil of Gerard de Lairesse (carried #529 spare), Gerrit Rademaker (1672-1711) and Jacob van Huysum (proposed spare 7); sexton of the Amstelkerk from 1718; cooperated with Picart in 1729; made the title plate for van Gool's Nieuw Schouburg (1750); a central figure of the Stadstekenacademie circle (Folkema, Morellon, Troost, Fokke, Elgersma, Tanje); works at the Westerkerk, Nieuwe Kerk and Town Hall; "exchanged brush for graver"; his collection was auctioned in 1776; Ploos ranked him with Isaac de Moucheron, de Wit and Troost. He is the reserve because he is the hinge between the painter's trade and the engraver's: the man who made the same crossing Punt made in reverse (Punt went from needle to brush late; Dubourg went from brush to graver), but his own engraved oeuvre is thin and his dates rest on one article. The doorway is the history painter who swapped the brush for the burin and taught the academy circle to etch. The doorway is the booksellers' engraver who put the whole Dutch history into small plates. Jakob van der Schley (26 July 1715 - 7 February 1779) - the boy who finished his dead master's plates and then mapped the world for the book trade. Pupil of Picart from about age 12; completed unfinished plates after Picart's death; made the memorial portrait of Picart (1734); maps for Prevost's Histoire generale des voyages (publisher Pieter de Hondt, The Hague); illustrations for La Vie de Marianne (1735-47); Brantome frontispieces (1740); L'Imprimerie (1739); taught Philippus van der Schley (1724-1817); cooperated with Prosper Marchand. The doorway is the boy who finished his dead master's plates and then mapped the world for the book trade. Jacobus Buys (19 November 1724 - 7 April 1801; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the wig-maker's son who studied under all three of Amsterdam's masters and ended up running its drawing academy. (The 14 names carried in #540 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt), Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem), Ludolf Bakhuizen (28 December 1630 or 1632, Emden - 7 November 1708, Amsterdam) (mains); Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695) (reserve); Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen), Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem) (alternates); spares: Aernout Smit (1641, Amsterdam - 1710), Michiel Maddersteg (bapt. 5 January 1662, Enkhuizen - d. 1708, Berlin), Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn), Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich), Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich), Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich), Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - after 1693), Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam). Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. German Baroque landscape and animal painter; family fled to Amsterdam in the Thirty Years' War (1640); trained with Guilliam du Gardijn, Cornelis de Bie and Barent Graat, but Berchem and Dujardin shaped him; worked for Ernst, Landgrave of Hesse-Rheinfels 1654-1659; court painter to Charles I Louis, Elector Palatine, 1664; moved to Frankfurt 1667; died in a house fire in 1685 trying to save his belongings. Specialised in pastoral idylls - idealized landscapes with ancient ruins, shepherds, sheep and cattle, the longing for harmony between men and animals and nature; never went to Italy, built his ruins from engravings; also one of the finest German portraitists of his time; many individual animal drawings (Albertina), intensifying the individuality of each species; Goethe called him the "Raphael of all livestock painters"; sons Philipp Peter Roos (Rosa di Tivoli, 1655-1706) and Johann Melchior Roos (1663-1731) were also painters. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the German pastoral before Kobell, the model Govert van der Leeuw painted "in the manner of" and one of the masters Kobell engraved after - the animal piece as an ideal, not a copy. The doorway is the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions as a study subject; no local file; not carried in #522-#539 as a subject. Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. Dutch painter and art dealer; winter landscapes, cityscapes, harbour scenes, beach views, river views, village scenes; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1664; may have been a pupil of Caesar van Everdingen (no influence of Everdingen visible - Heeremans was purely a landscape painter, not a figure painter); style shaped by Klaes Molenaer, but brighter in palette and livelier in movement, villagers skating, sledging, fishing and talking; frequently returned to Scheveningen beach and Egmond village, beach and ruins; pictures often go in pairs; collaborated with specialist figure painters - Abraham Storck painted the figures in Winter landscape with the Montelbaanstoren, Amsterdam; signed "THMANS" with the letters run together, so dealers long misread him as "Frederik Hendrik Mans". He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the staffage problem from the other side: a landscapist who hired the figures in, the mirror of van de Velde the hired staffage hand. The doorway is the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. FLAG: the page says earliest dated work 1660 and last dated work 1695, against burial 24 January 1694 - the 1695 date cannot stand as painted in his lifetime; also the 1659 "pupil of an Everdingen" record is assumed to be Caesar van Everdingen, not proven. Ludolf Bakhuizen (28 December 1630 or 1632, Emden - 7 November 1708, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine painter who took the sea when the van de Veldes left. German-born Dutch painter, draughtsman, calligrapher and printmaker, the leading Dutch marine painter after both Willem van de Veldes left for England in 1672. He came to Amsterdam around 1650 as a merchant's clerk, taught himself to draw ships, studied under Allaert van Everdingen and Hendrick Dubbels, joined the painters' guild in 1663, and became famous for violent water - he went out to sea in an open boat to study storms. He painted the great View of Amsterdam and the IJ (1665) as a present for Louis XIV's minister, opened a gallery on the top floor of the Amsterdam town hall in 1699, and was visited by Cosimo III de' Medici and Peter the Great. His pupils and followers include Jan Claesz Rietschoof, Aernout Smit, Michiel Maddersteg and Abraham Storck. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the hub of the whole marine spare set - three of this study's spares are his pupils or followers - so studying him unlocks all of them, and his open-boat storm study is a mechanism no other candidate offers. The doorway is the clerk-turned-calligrapher who rowed out into storms in an open boat to learn how water breaks, and then taught half of Amsterdam's marine painters. Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter; lent his perspective books to Johannes Verkolje, who became better at perspective drawing than he was; teacher of his son Jacob II (Coning) and his younger brother Philips Koninck (carried #537); uncle of Salomon Koninck; in Dordrecht 1633-1636, Rotterdam 1637-1645, The Hague 1647-1651, Amsterdam 1658, Copenhagen by c.1676, painter to the court of Christian V of Denmark. He is the reserve because he is the hinge of the whole Koninck family and a second van de Velde disciple whose line runs to Copenhagen, but his own oeuvre is thin and his dates are the shakiest in this block. The doorway is the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Dutch-Danish painter to the Danish Court; son of Jacob Koninck; trained under his father and Adriaen van de Velde - a fellow pupil of van Bergen by the same master, and the #538 pupil list's fourth name; Copenhagen 1676, court work from 1680 (contact possibly through his wife, who worked for Queen Charlotte Amalie); one of the first painters in Denmark-Norway to specialise in topographical painting; Norway 1698-1699 for Frederiksberg Palace; with Thomas Quellinus, Hendrik Krock and others proposed an art academy in Copenhagen on 6 October 1701; also a portraitist to Danish and Norwegian nobility; despite court contact he died in the poorhouse of the Reformed Church in Store Kongensgade after repeated forced auctions. The doorway is the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter and draughtsman; son of tailor Jan Mienssen Molenaer, brother of Bartholomeus and Jan Miense Molenaer (carried #535 main); Roman Catholic family, so no baptismal register and no exact birth date; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1651, dues paid yearly until 1676; winter landscape painter influenced by Jacob van Ruisdael; frozen rivers teeming with skaters, sleds, horse-drawn sleighs, a church and an inn under low winter sun; taught by the little-known Nicolaes Piemont; earliest dated work 1644; works in the Rijksmuseum, Boijmans Van Beuningen, Schwerin, Braunschweig. The doorway is the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. These look like two Molenaers conflated under one name; do not mix them. Aernout Smit (1641, Amsterdam - 1710; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the attic painter who copied Bakhuizen on the record. Marine painter; pupil of Jan Theunisz Blanckerhoff; painted in an attic room on the Singel, probably employed by dealer Laurens Cornelisz de Coninck; follower and copyist of Ludolf Bakhuizen (1687/1689 notarial records of copying Bakhuizen); The Africa in Table Bay; most marines 1667-1678, later style tending to Bakhuizen; Amsterdam Guild of St Luke 1688; died aged about 70, buried at the Leiden cemetery. The doorway is the attic painter who copied Bakhuizen on the record. FLAG: birth 1640/41 per a 1667 document giving his age as "about 26"; John Bennett gives Bakhuizen as "1631-1701", which is wrong (1708). Michiel Maddersteg (bapt. 5 January 1662, Enkhuizen - d. 1708, Berlin; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine painter who built his patron real ships. Marine painter; studied under Ludolf Bakhuizen; spent most of his life at the court of Frederick I of Prussia, returned to Amsterdam later in life, where he commissioned the building of two beautiful ships for Frederick I's pleasure; most of his paintings are in Germany. The doorway is the marine painter who built his patron real ships. Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Hoorn pupil who copied his master's storm the year after it was painted. Dutch Golden Age seascape painter; pupil of Abraham Liedts, then of Ludolf Bakhuizen; active in Hoorn all his career, possibly some time in Amsterdam with Bakhuizen; taught his son Hendrik; one of Bakhuizen's most accomplished pupils (Sotheby's, on a stormy sea after Bakhuizen's Fishing Vessels Offshore in a Heavy Sea, 1684, completed 1685); works in the Rijksmuseum, Het Scheepvaartmuseum, Westfries Museum Hoorn, the Hermitage, the National Maritime Museum Greenwich; Hoorn contemporary of the flower painter Jacob Rootius, son of Jan Albertsz Rotius. The doorway is the Hoorn pupil who copied his master's storm the year after it was painted. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. FLAG: son Hendrik's death 1747 (father's page, artsdot) vs 1746 (son's own page, Wikipedia). Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. German painter and copper engraver; grandson of Johann Heinrich Kobell, grocer of Frankfurt who settled in Mannheim in 1720; studied law at Heidelberg, then painting under Peter Verschatfelt; court painter 1766; Paris 1768-1769 under Johann Georg Wille; followed Elector Charles Theodore to Munich 1794; named Director of the Palatinate Painting Gallery 1798 but died before taking up the post; landscapes in the style of Berchem; about 300 etchings, published as Oeuvres completes (1809), marking a distinct advance in German landscape etching; father of Wilhelm, brother of Franz. The doorway is the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. Both may be true at different stages; no source read reconciles them. Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the uncle who drew twenty thousand landscapes because paint was too slow. German painter, etcher and draftsman; brother of Ferdinand, uncle and teacher of Wilhelm; sent to Italy by Elector Karl Theodor 1776, in Rome until 1785, working from nature and monumental buildings; court painter at Munich; produced only a few oil paintings, highly praised by Goethe (Rocky Landscape with Waterfalls, once in the Bamberg gallery), but left 20,000 landscape and architecture pen drawings and etchings, painting being too slow to keep pace with his ideas. The doorway is the uncle who drew twenty thousand landscapes because paint was too slow. Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the professor Kobell replaced, who taught Munich to paint what it saw. German landscape painter, pioneer of Naturalism; trained under Johann Jakob Dorner the elder and at the Munich Zeichnungsakademie; inspector of the Hofgarten Galerie 1790; professor of landscape painting at the Munich Academy 1808-1814, replaced by Wilhelm von Kobell in 1813/1814; central gallery director 1822, founding director of what became the Alte Pinakothek; mentor of Carl Rottmann and Ludwig Richter; insisted on Naturstudium, direct study of nature, against the history-painting hierarchy. The doorway is the professor Kobell replaced, who taught Munich to paint what it saw. Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - after 1693; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Dutch Italianate landscape painter and printmaker; pupil of Nicolaes Berchem around 1642; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1646; sun-drenched southern landscapes with shepherds, cattle, sheep and goats resting by rivers, long shadows, hazy golden light; last dated works 1694; Kobell etched after him (The Shepherd's Rest, 1819, after Romeyn). The doorway is the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Dutch Golden Age painter, pupil of Rembrandt; orphan, parents from Metz; apprenticed to Rembrandt from 1 November 1629, six signed receipts for 1630-1631, travelled with him to Amsterdam in 1631; enrolled at Leiden University April 1632; married Maria le Febure 1636; Deventer 1641; back in Amsterdam 1643, died young in 1645; his widow married glassmaker Pieter de Melder in 1648, and his daughter Mariecke later married Frederik de Moucheron - the Jouderville line marries straight into the Moucheron line; painted mainly Rembrandtesque heads or tronies, so faithful that several of his paintings were previously attributed to Rembrandt; Man in Oriental Costume was the Fake or Fortune? case (BBC, 2011), a Nazi-seized Oppenheimer stock picture. The doorway is the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Extra verified spares (not part of the 8 above, all verified NOT STUDIED): Jan Baptist Boel the Younger (between September and 4 October 1643, Antwerp - 9 January 1689; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S22). Son and pupil of Pieter Boel; family moved to Paris 1668 for the Gobelins; married Maria Catharina Immenraet, daughter of landscape painter Philips Augustijn Immenraet, 4 May 1675; collaborated with other Antwerp painters, adding the still-life and animal elements to their compositions; known oeuvre very limited; assimilated his father's style so completely that attribution problems follow (Nationalmuseum). Held back because his oeuvre is small and mostly indistinguishable from his father's. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#539. FLAG: the Wikipedia lead says 9 January 1689, the body says 1 January 1689 - an internal conflict in the same article. Jan Theunisz Blanckerhoff (also Blankerhoff/Blankhof/Jan Maat; 10 January 1628, Alkmaar - bur. 2 October 1669, Amsterdam; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S26). Marine painter; taught by Arent Teerling, Pieter Scheyenburg and Caesar van Everdingen; served on a Dutch warship; Rome, Bentvueghels, called Jan Maat; Italian ports with strange boats his most popular pieces; teacher of Aernout Smit; Battle of the Zuiderzee 1663-1668, 700 guilders, Rijksmuseum SK-A-3235. Held back because his career is short (work period 1649-1669) and his record is thin. Daniel de Koninck (1668, Amsterdam - 1730, England; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S27). Nephew of Philips Koninck; apprenticed to his uncle Jacob Koninck at the Copenhagen court 1687-1690; moved to England 1690 (Oxford, then London by 1720); genre works and portraits/tronies in the manner of Rembrandt; Peter King, 1st Baron King of Ockham (1720, National Portrait Gallery). Held back because he is a portraitist, off the landscape line. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#539. Hendrik Kobell (13 September 1751, Rotterdam - 3 August 1779, Rotterdam; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S29). Dutch landscape and marine painter, etcher, draftsman, watercolourist; cousin of Ferdinand and Franz Kobell; father trained him in drawing, he loved drawing ships in Rotterdam port; London 1769-1770, Paris 1772, Amsterdam Stadstekenakademie; founder of the Rotterdam drawing society Hierdoor tot Hooger; father and teacher of Jan Kobell II (1778-1814); pupils Carel Frederik Bendorp and Gerrit van der Pals; The Shipwreck (1775, Rijksmuseum SK-A-1218). Held back because he died at 27 and the record is short. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#539. Younger son of Dominic Serres; landscape painter (the only Serres who left the sea); served in the Royal Navy on HMS Foudroyant 1782; married Lucretia Madden 4 August 1788; prisoner of the King's Bench Prison by December 1813; died 1816; View of the Teign, Devon (GAC). New names from Kobell's own network -- verified NOT STUDIED, for a later round (NOT part of the 8 extra spares above): Carl Rottmann (11 January 1797, Handschuhsheim - 7 July 1850, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source). German landscape painter, most famous of the Rottmann family. First drawing lessons from his father Friedrich, who taught drawing at Heidelberg University; moved to Munich in 1821/1822, married Friederike von Sckell in 1824, and through her came to the attention of Crown Prince Ludwig, who sent him to Italy in 1826/1827 to sketch the 28 Italian landscapes in fresco for the Hofgarten arcades (completed 1833), then commissioned a Greek cycle in 1834 (23 large landscapes, now in the Neue Pinakothek). Court painter in 1841. His school included Karl Lindemann-Frommel, Karl Ludwig Seeger, August Loffler and August Seidel. WHY: the Munich landscapist who raised landscape to the height of history painting in fresco - the direct successor to Dillis's and Kobell's Munich. The doorway is: from Dillis's Munich, hop to the painter Ludwig sent to Italy to fresco a whole arcade of landscapes. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file (parent verified both). Peter von Cornelius (23 September 1783, Dusseldorf - 6 March 1867, Berlin; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source). German painter, a main representative of the Nazarene movement. Drawing classes at the Dusseldorf Academy from age twelve; Rome 1811-1819 with the Lukasbund; summoned to Munich by Crown Prince Ludwig in 1819 to decorate the Glyptothek; director of the Dusseldorf Academy in 1821 and of the Munich Academy from 1824/1825; his "Last Judgment" (1829-1840) fills the east wall of the Ludwigskirche in Munich, 19 m by 12 m. His pupils included Karl Hermann, Wilhelm von Kaulbach and Adam Eberle; he left Munich for Berlin in 1839-1840. WHY: the academy director who rebuilt German painting around fresco and the Munich school - the institutional successor to Kobell's generation. The doorway is: from Kobell's Munich Academy, hop to the Nazarene who took its directorship and covered a church wall with the Last Judgment. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file (the file cornelius-cardew.md in the studies directory is Cornelius Cardew, a different person; parent verified both).



(The 14 names carried in #539 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Jacob de Wit (bapt. 19 December 1695, Amsterdam - d. 12 November 1754, Amsterdam), Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt), Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem) (mains); Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695) (reserve); Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen), Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem) (alternates); spares: Aernout Smit (1641, Amsterdam - 1710), Michiel Maddersteg (bapt. 5 January 1662, Enkhuizen - d. 1708, Berlin), Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn), Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich), Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich), Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich), Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - 1694, Haarlem), Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam). Jacob de Wit (bapt. 19 December 1695, Amsterdam - d. 12 November 1754, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the man who painted stone that was not there, on the ceilings of Amsterdam. Dutch Rococo painter and interior decorator; pupil of Albert van Spiers and Jacob van Hal, Antwerp Guild of St Luke 1714; copied Rubens's ceilings in the Carolus Borromeuskerk in watercolour 1711-1722, the record after lightning destroyed them in 1718 (engraved by his pupil Jan Punt, 1751); famous for door and ceiling paintings in Amsterdam canal houses (he lived on the Keizersgracht) and country houses on the Vecht; grisailles he called "witjes" - grey-white paintings faking stone relief; Moses Choosing the Seventy Elders (1737, Amsterdam Town Hall), Apotheosis of Aeneas for Pieter Pels (Herengracht 479), ceiling with Isaac de Moucheron; pupils Jan Punt, Pieter Tanje, Dionys van Nijmegen, Jan de Groot, Frans and Jacob Xavery; follower Tako Hajo Jelgersma. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the Moucheron line's other half - the figure painter inside the wall-painter's room - and the witje is the one trompe-l'oeil mechanism this chain has never studied. The doorway is the man who painted stone that was not there, on the ceilings of Amsterdam. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. FLAG: one conservation page heads him "(1685-1754)" while its own body gives baptized 19 December 1695 - the 1685 is a typo. Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. German Baroque landscape and animal painter; family fled to Amsterdam in the Thirty Years' War (1640); trained with Guilliam du Gardijn, Cornelis de Bie and Barent Graat, but Berchem and Dujardin shaped him; worked for Ernst, Landgrave of Hesse-Rheinfels 1654-1659; court painter to Charles I Louis, Elector Palatine, 1664; moved to Frankfurt 1667; died in a house fire in 1685 trying to save his belongings. Specialised in pastoral idylls - idealized landscapes with ancient ruins, shepherds, sheep and cattle, the longing for harmony between men and animals and nature; never went to Italy, built his ruins from engravings; also one of the finest German portraitists of his time; many individual animal drawings (Albertina), intensifying the individuality of each species; Goethe called him the "Raphael of all livestock painters"; sons Philipp Peter Roos (Rosa di Tivoli, 1655-1706) and Johann Melchior Roos (1663-1731) were also painters. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the German pastoral before Kobell, the model Govert van der Leeuw painted "in the manner of" and one of the masters Kobell engraved after - the animal piece as an ideal, not a copy. The doorway is the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. Dutch painter and art dealer; winter landscapes, cityscapes, harbour scenes, beach views, river views, village scenes; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1664; may have been a pupil of Caesar van Everdingen (no influence of Everdingen visible - Heeremans was purely a landscape painter, not a figure painter); style shaped by Klaes Molenaer, but brighter in palette and livelier in movement, villagers skating, sledging, fishing and talking; frequently returned to Scheveningen beach and Egmond village, beach and ruins; pictures often go in pairs; collaborated with specialist figure painters - Abraham Storck painted the figures in Winter landscape with the Montelbaanstoren, Amsterdam; signed "THMANS" with the letters run together, so dealers long misread him as "Frederik Hendrik Mans". He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the staffage problem from the other side: a landscapist who hired the figures in, the mirror of van de Velde the hired staffage hand. The doorway is the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. FLAG: the page says earliest dated work 1660 and last dated work 1695, against burial 24 January 1694 - the 1695 date cannot stand as painted in his lifetime; also the 1659 "pupil of an Everdingen" record is assumed to be Caesar van Everdingen, not proven. Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter; lent his perspective books to Johannes Verkolje, who became better at perspective drawing than he was; teacher of his son Jacob II (Coning) and his younger brother Philips Koninck (carried #537); uncle of Salomon Koninck; in Dordrecht 1633-1636, Rotterdam 1637-1645, The Hague 1647-1651, Amsterdam 1658, Copenhagen by c.1676, painter to the court of Christian V of Denmark. He is the reserve because he is the hinge of the whole Koninck family and a second van de Velde disciple whose line runs to Copenhagen, but his own oeuvre is thin and his dates are the shakiest in this block. The doorway is the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. FLAG: teacher conflict - Houbraken says disciple of Adriaen van de Velde, RKD says pupil of David Colijns; death c.1695 (Wikipedia) vs 1666 (artsdot, wrong) vs 1690 or later (British Museum); birth c.1612 (Sphinx) vs c.1615 (Wikipedia/British Museum 1614/15). Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Dutch-Danish painter to the Danish Court; son of Jacob Koninck; trained under his father and Adriaen van de Velde - a fellow pupil of van Bergen by the same master, and the #538 pupil list's fourth name; Copenhagen 1676, court work from 1680 (contact possibly through his wife, who worked for Queen Charlotte Amalie); one of the first painters in Denmark-Norway to specialise in topographical painting; Norway 1698-1699 for Frederiksberg Palace; with Thomas Quellinus, Hendrik Krock and others proposed an art academy in Copenhagen on 6 October 1701; also a portraitist to Danish and Norwegian nobility; despite court contact he died in the poorhouse of the Reformed Church in Store Kongensgade after repeated forced auctions. The doorway is the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter and draughtsman; son of tailor Jan Mienssen Molenaer, brother of Bartholomeus and Jan Miense Molenaer (carried #535 main); Roman Catholic family, so no baptismal register and no exact birth date; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1651, dues paid yearly until 1676; winter landscape painter influenced by Jacob van Ruisdael; frozen rivers teeming with skaters, sleds, horse-drawn sleighs, a church and an inn under low winter sun; taught by the little-known Nicolaes Piemont; earliest dated work 1644; works in the Rijksmuseum, Boijmans Van Beuningen, Schwerin, Braunschweig. The doorway is the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Hoorn pupil who copied his master's storm the year after it was painted. Dutch Golden Age seascape painter; pupil of Abraham Liedts, then of Ludolf Bakhuizen; active in Hoorn all his career, possibly some time in Amsterdam with Bakhuizen; taught his son Hendrik; one of Bakhuizen's most accomplished pupils (Sotheby's, on a stormy sea after Bakhuizen's Fishing Vessels Offshore in a Heavy Sea, 1684, completed 1685); works in the Rijksmuseum, Het Scheepvaartmuseum, Westfries Museum Hoorn, the Hermitage, the National Maritime Museum Greenwich; Hoorn contemporary of the flower painter Jacob Rootius, son of Jan Albertsz Rotius. The doorway is the Hoorn pupil who copied his master's storm the year after it was painted. Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. German painter and copper engraver; grandson of Johann Heinrich Kobell, grocer of Frankfurt who settled in Mannheim in 1720; studied law at Heidelberg, then painting under Peter Verschatfelt; court painter 1766; Paris 1768-1769 under Johann Georg Wille; followed Elector Charles Theodore to Munich 1794; named Director of the Palatinate Painting Gallery 1798 but died before taking up the post; landscapes in the style of Berchem; about 300 etchings, published as Oeuvres completes (1809), marking a distinct advance in German landscape etching; father of Wilhelm, brother of Franz. The doorway is the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the uncle who drew twenty thousand landscapes because paint was too slow. German painter, etcher and draftsman; brother of Ferdinand, uncle and teacher of Wilhelm; sent to Italy by Elector Karl Theodor 1776, in Rome until 1785, working from nature and monumental buildings; court painter at Munich; produced only a few oil paintings, highly praised by Goethe (Rocky Landscape with Waterfalls, once in the Bamberg gallery), but left 20,000 landscape and architecture pen drawings and etchings, painting being too slow to keep pace with his ideas. The doorway is the uncle who drew twenty thousand landscapes because paint was too slow. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the professor Kobell replaced, who taught Munich to paint what it saw. German landscape painter, pioneer of Naturalism; trained under Johann Jakob Dorner the elder and at the Munich Zeichnungsakademie; inspector of the Hofgarten Galerie 1790; professor of landscape painting at the Munich Academy 1808-1814, replaced by Wilhelm von Kobell in 1813/1814; central gallery director 1822, founding director of what became the Alte Pinakothek; mentor of Carl Rottmann and Ludwig Richter; insisted on Naturstudium, direct study of nature, against the history-painting hierarchy. The doorway is the professor Kobell replaced, who taught Munich to paint what it saw. Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - 1694, Haarlem; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Dutch Italianate landscape painter and printmaker; pupil of Nicolaes Berchem around 1642; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1646; sun-drenched southern landscapes with shepherds, cattle, sheep and goats resting by rivers, long shadows, hazy golden light; last dated works 1694; Kobell etched after him (The Shepherd's Rest, 1819, after Romeyn). The doorway is the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Dutch Golden Age painter, pupil of Rembrandt; orphan, parents from Metz; apprenticed to Rembrandt from 1 November 1629, six signed receipts for 1630-1631, travelled with him to Amsterdam in 1631; enrolled at Leiden University April 1632; married Maria le Febure 1636; Deventer 1641; back in Amsterdam 1643, died young in 1645; his widow married glassmaker Pieter de Melder in 1648, and his daughter Mariecke later married Frederik de Moucheron - the Jouderville line marries straight into the Moucheron line; painted mainly Rembrandtesque heads or tronies, so faithful that several of his paintings were previously attributed to Rembrandt; Man in Oriental Costume was the Fake or Fortune? case (BBC, 2011), a Nazi-seized Oppenheimer stock picture. The doorway is the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Extra verified spares (not part of the 8 above, all verified NOT STUDIED): Jan Baptist Boel the Younger (between September and 4 October 1643, Antwerp - 9 January 1689; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S22). Son and pupil of Pieter Boel; family moved to Paris 1668 for the Gobelins; married Maria Catharina Immenraet, daughter of landscape painter Philips Augustijn Immenraet, 4 May 1675; collaborated with other Antwerp painters, adding the still-life and animal elements to their compositions; known oeuvre very limited; assimilated his father's style so completely that attribution problems follow (Nationalmuseum). Held back because his oeuvre is small and mostly indistinguishable from his father's. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Jan Theunisz Blanckerhoff (also Blankerhoff/Blankhof/Jan Maat; 10 January 1628, Alkmaar - bur. 2 October 1669, Amsterdam; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S26). Marine painter; taught by Arent Teerling, Pieter Scheyenburg and Caesar van Everdingen; served on a Dutch warship; Rome, Bentvueghels, called Jan Maat; Italian ports with strange boats his most popular pieces; teacher of Aernout Smit; Battle of the Zuiderzee 1663-1668, 700 guilders, Rijksmuseum SK-A-3235. Held back because his career is short (work period 1649-1669) and his record is thin. Held back because he is a portraitist, off the landscape line. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Hendrik Kobell (13 September 1751, Rotterdam - 3 August 1779, Rotterdam; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S29). Dutch landscape and marine painter, etcher, draftsman, watercolourist; cousin of Ferdinand and Franz Kobell; father trained him in drawing, he loved drawing ships in Rotterdam port; London 1769-1770, Paris 1772, Amsterdam Stadstekenakademie; founder of the Rotterdam drawing society Hierdoor tot Hooger; father and teacher of Jan Kobell II (1778-1814); pupils Carel Frederik Bendorp and Gerrit van der Pals; The Shipwreck (1775, Rijksmuseum SK-A-1218). Held back because he died at 27 and the record is short. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538.
