Ludolf Bakhuizen (28 December 1630 - 7 November 1708; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine painter who took the sea when the van de Veldes left: clerk and calligrapher turned painter, student of Everdingen then Dubbels, who went out in an open boat to study storms and became the leading Dutch marine painter after 1672. He is main 2 rather than top pick because the hop runs through the pupil first, and Bakhuizen is the sea line, not the cattle line. The doorway is the clerk who went out in an open boat to study storms. He is main 3 rather than top pick because the hop runs through the pupil and the marine line, not through the menagerie. The doorway is the man who painted from live animals at Versailles. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is Boel's fellow pupil of the menagerie line, and the study should meet Boel first. The doorway is the Snyders pupil who drew 52 species for the king. Reserve: Jan van de Cappelle (bapt. 25 January 1626 - bur. 22 December 1679; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the calm-water mirror: dyeworks heir who never needed to sell a picture, painted fewer than 150 works in which dead-calm water mirrors the clouds; collector of 192 paintings and over 7,000 drawings, the only man painted by both Rembrandt and Frans Hals; the man who taught Willem the Younger how to paint a sky reflected in water. He is the reserve because his mechanism set (the mirror water, the collector as painter) is the most specialised in the network, a strong contrast study whenever the chain wants one. February 1628 - in/after 1685), Willem van de Velde the Younger (bapt. 18 December 1633 - 6 April 1707) (alternates); spares: Gerrit Berckheyde (bapt. 6 June 1638 - 10 June 1698), Pieter de Molijn (bapt. 6 April 1595 - 23 March 1661), Caesar van Everdingen (1616/17 - bur. 13 October 1678), Pieter de Grebber (c. 1600 - 1652/53), Willem Cornelisz Duyster (1599-1635), Salomon van Ruysdael (c. 1602 - bur. 3 November 1670), Willem van de Velde the Elder (c. 1611 - 13 December 1693), Jan Fyt (bapt. 15 March 1611 - 11 September 1661). Jan Weenix (1642-1719; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the hunt line carried into dead-game still life. Trained by his father Jan Baptist Weenix (carried #531) with his cousin Melchior d'Hondecoeter; dead game and hunting scenes; Utrecht guild 1664 and 1668; painted Peter the Great 1697; court painter to Elector Palatine Johann Wilhelm 1702-1712/14, 12 large hunting pictures for Schloss Bensberg; pupils his daughter Maria Weenix and Dirk Valkenburg (carried #536 extra spare); Goethe devoted a poem to his animal textures; the Wallace Collection holds 13 paintings. He is main 2 rather than top pick because the copy line runs through the cattle painter first, and Weenix is the still-life end of the hunt, not its living middle. The doorway is the hunter who became the still-life painter of dead game. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. FLAG: birth year uncertain - sources give between 1641 and 1649; 1642 is the commonly cited year; the page notes he married in 1679 "around thirty" and had 13 children 1680-1700. Frans Snyders (11 November 1579 - 19 August 1657; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Antwerp root of the whole hunt and animal line. Pupil of Pieter Brueghel the Younger 1593, then Hendrick van Balen; master 1602; Italy 1608-09 (Rome, Milan, Cardinal Borromeo); married Margaretha de Vos 1611 (sister of Cornelis and Paul de Vos); Rubens collaboration from the 1610s; Torre de la Parada 1636-38, about 60 hunting and animal pieces after Rubens designs, plus 18 more 1639; dean 1628; pupils Nicasius Bernaerts, Peter van Boucle, Juriaen Jacobsze, Jan Roos, Paul de Vos; Jan Fyt student and assistant from 1629; patrons Archduke Albert VII, Philip IV of Spain, Bishop Antonius Triest; influenced Jan Davidsz. de Heem and the 18th-century French animaliers Oudry and Desportes; the concert of birds theme; died childless. He is main 3 rather than top pick because the hop runs through the son's copying, not through Snyders directly, and his scale would swamp the Haarlem chain. The doorway is the Antwerp root of the hunt. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Paul de Vos (1591/92 or 1595 - 30 June 1678; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Snyders's brother-in-law and workshop colleague; the second Antwerp hunt painter. Pupil of Denijs van Hove 1604, David Remeeus 1605, Eduard Snayers; worked in his brother-in-law Frans Snyders' workshop (Snyders married his sister Margaretha 1611); guild master 1620; monumental hunts for aristocrats, warmer colours and broader brush than Snyders, fighting cats, horses attacked by wolves; collaborator with Rubens (Torre de la Parada), Van Dyck, Jan Wildens; patrons Marquis of Leganes and Philippe-Charles 3rd Count of Arenberg (36+ paintings 1633-1640); many works long misattributed to Snyders; never dated his paintings. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is Snyders's shadow, and the study should meet Snyders first. The doorway is the brother-in-law in Snyders's shadow who painted the fighting cats. Reserve: Melchior d'Hondecoeter (c. 1636 - 3 April 1695; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the bird painter: grandson of Gillis, son of Gijsbert, nephew of J. B. Weenix, cousin of Jan Weenix; trained by his father Gijsbert then his uncle J. B. Weenix; Confrerie Pictura 1659; married Susanne Tradel 1663; painted almost exclusively birds (exotic and game) in park landscapes; about 250 works, only about 20 dated; pupils Willem Hendrik Wilhelmus van Royen and Jan Weenix; employed by William III to paint his menagerie at Het Loo; his inventory lists a small gallows (to pose birds) and seven Snyders paintings; followers include Adriaen Coorte, Jan van Huysum, Elias Vonck; early sea-pieces and Tub with Fish 1655 before switching to birds. He is the reserve because his mechanism set (the bird as still life in a park landscape, the menagerie as subject) is the most specialised in the network, a strong contrast study whenever the chain wants one. The doorway is the bird painter with a gallows in his inventory. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. The doorway is the forest painter whose Swiss lake was mistaken for Italy. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. Willem van de Velde the Younger (bapt. 18 December 1633 - 6 April 1707; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine line: son of Willem the Elder, elder brother of Adriaen; trained by his father then Simon de Vlieger (Weesp); Amsterdam until 1672, then England with his father, Charles II service, studio at Queen's House Greenwich, Westminster 1691; leading Dutch marine painter of the later 17th century. He is an alternate rather than a main because the marine line is a step sideways from the hunt, and his brother is the top pick. The doorway is the elder brother who painted the sea while the younger painted the cattle. His is the study of the Haarlem townscape painter who also painted cavalry pieces, the town answer to Oudendijck's camp. The doorway is the Haarlem townscape painter the son followed. Pieter de Molijn (bapt. 6 April 1595 London - 23 March 1661 Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Guild Haarlem 1616, dean 1633, 1638, 1646; tonal-phase pioneer with Jan van Goyen and Salomon van Ruysdael; pupils Gerard ter Borch the Younger and Elder, Allaert van Everdingen, Jan Wils, Jan Coelenbier; collaborated with ter Borch, Frans Hals, Pieter de Grebber, Jacob de Wet, Jacob Pynas, Nicolaes Berchem; son Anthony de Molijn (1635-c. 1703). His is the study of the tonal pioneer who taught the generation before Oudendijck. The doorway is the tonal pioneer who taught ter Borch and Everdingen. His is the study of the father who drew sea battles in pen while his sons painted them in oil. The doorway is the father who sketched the fleet in pen. Jan Fyt (bapt. 15 March 1611 - 11 September 1661; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Snyders's pupil and assistant 1629-31, master 1629/30; Italy 1633-41 (Paris, Venice for the Sagredo and Contarini families, Rome 1635, Bentvueghels bent-name "Goudvink", bullfinch); back in Antwerp by 1641; Guild of Romanists dean 1652; married Francoise van de Sande 22 March 1654, four children; about 380 paintings (287 extant still lifes per Christie's); moved the dead-game display from the indoor table to the open landscape, the first to paint game as a trophy of the hunt rather than food; pupils Pieter Boel and Jacob van der Kerckhoven; collaborators Cornelius Schut, Thomas Willeboirts Bosschaert, Erasmus Quellinus II; Set of the Dogs 1642 dedicated to Don Carlo Guasco, Marquess of Soleno. His is the study of the man who took dead game out of the kitchen and into the landscape. The doorway is the pupil who moved the game from the table to the field. Jan Porcellis (1580/84 Ghent - 29 January 1632 Zoeterwoude; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - marine painter; initiated the decisive transition from early realism to the tonal phase: overcast skies, rough waters, low horizons, two-thirds of the canvas sea and sky; supposed pupil of Hendrick Vroom (per Houbraken); Antwerp guild 1617, Rotterdam by 1615, Haarlem around 1622; teacher of Simon de Vlieger per the Royal Museums Greenwich; the most influential marine painter of the first half of the 17th century; only about ten works securely identified. Hercules Segers (c. 1589 - c. 1638; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Haarlem-born; pupil of Gillis van Coninxloo; the most inspired, experimental and original landscapist of his period and an even more innovative printmaker; 54 plates, 183 known impressions, about 15 paintings; printed in coloured ink on coloured paper and cloth, hand-coloured, cropped to different sizes, each impression a miniature painting; invented lift-ground (sugar-bite) etching, not used again for 150 years; Rembrandt owned eight of his paintings and a printing plate; art dealer by trade; wife recorded as a widow in 1638. (The 14 names carried in #536 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Salomon de Bray (1597 - 11 May 1664), Leendert van der Cooghen (9 May 1632 - 18 February 1681), Dirk Maas (12 September 1659 - 25 December 1717) (mains); Jan van Huchtenburg (bapt. 20 November 1647 - bur. 2 July 1733) (reserve); Jan Wyck (29 October 1645 - 17 May 1702), Jacob Gole (1660-1737) (alternates); spares: Egbert van Heemskerck the Elder (1634 - c. 1702/04), Evert Collier (26 January 1642 - few days before 8 September 1708), Adriaen Oudendijck (1677-1704), Jacob Jordaens (19 May 1593 - 18 October 1678), Rachel Ruysch (3 June 1664 - 12 October 1750), Jan van Gool (1685-1763), Gerrit Claesz Bleker (1592 - 8 February 1656), John Wootton (c. 1682 - 13 November 1764). Salomon de Bray (1597 - 11 May 1664; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the draftsman whose figure studies Dusart kept: they sit in Dusart's 1704 probate inventory alongside Bega's 125 figure studies, so Dusart the collector valued de Bray's drawing as highly as his own master's circle. He is also the Haarlem classicist against whom the whole peasant line defines itself: history painter, architect of the City Hall and the Zijlpoort, town planner, poet, designer of silverwork, author of Architectura Moderna (1631), headman of the Guild of St. Luke who tried and failed to rewrite its charter in 1631 to put painting above the crafts. He died of plague in 1664 with four of his children; his sons Jan, Dirck and Joseph carried the name on, Jan already carried as a #535 spare. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he predates Dusart by a generation and the hop runs through the probate inventory, not through a shared master. The doorway is the classicist whose figure studies Dusart kept in his own inventory. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#535. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Leendert van der Cooghen (9 May 1632 - 18 February 1681; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the other draftsman in Dusart's probate inventory, and the closest parallel to Dusart himself as a draughtsman: an amateur of independent means who never had to sell, about 66 drawings and only 3 certain paintings, figure and portrait studies in red and black chalk drawn directly from the life model, dated day-month-year on 46 sheets so a chronology can be built, moving from a static dry style to a looser freer one. He trained with Jacob Jordaens, shared a studio with Cornelis Bega, befriended Vincent van der Vinne, and never married - "uncle Leendert" of the Schagelstraat, the bachelor counterpart to Dusart the bachelor who lived with his aunts. His mechanism set (the dated single-figure chalk study, the amateur who draws only when he feels like it) is the direct mirror of Dusart's own single-peasant chalks. The doorway is the amateur whose dated chalk studies Dusart collected and whose life mirrors his own. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#535. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Dirk Maas (12 September 1659 - 25 December 1717; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the pupil-chain hop with a direct Dusart document behind it: he was back in Haarlem in May 1692 on the testimony of Cornelis Dusart himself (Bredius), and he is the painter who took the Haarlem line to war - pupil of Mommers, then Berchem, then Jan van Huchtenburg, then painter of the Battle of the Boyne first-hand in 1690 for William III and for his patron Hans Willem Bentinck, Earl of Portland. He also did staffage for other painters and worked in the same studio as Adriaen Oudendijck 1695-1700, so he ties the Oudendijck top pick to the Huchtenburg reserve and the Wyck alternate in one knot. The doorway is the horse painter Dusart himself testified for, who painted the Boyne first-hand. Reserve: Jan van Huchtenburg (bapt. 20 November 1647 - bur. 2 July 1733; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the battle machine of the network: first taught by Thomas Wijck, then trained in Paris under Adam Frans van der Meulen at the Gobelins, then court painter to Prince Eugene of Savoy with a ten-canvas series of Eugene's battles from Zenta (1697) to Belgrade (1717), plus William III's campaigns. He taught Dirk Maas, collaborated with Jan Wyck, assisted Gerrit Berckheyde by painting his people and horses, and his pupils Alexander van Gaelen and Izaak Vogelensanck carried the Wouwerman battle style into England. He is the reserve because his mechanism set (the monumental multi-figure battle composition, the court-painter campaign series) is the largest scale in the network, a strong contrast study whenever the chain wants one. The doorway is the court painter who turned ten of Prince Eugene's battles into one series. Alternates if any of the 4 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Jan Wyck (29 October 1645 - 17 May 1702; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the son of Thomas Wijck who carried the Haarlem line to England and founded British military and sporting painting: over 150 works, the Battle of the Boyne (1690) painted half a dozen times, William III's campaigns through the Nine Years' War, and as a teacher the master of John Wootton and Martin Beckman. He accompanied Dirk Maas to Ireland to paint William's campaigns, and his battle pieces bring the viewer into the fight at eye level instead of the prevailing bird's-eye disposition of troops. He is an alternate rather than a main because the date conflict needs settling before he can be a top pick. The doorway is the Haarlem battle painter who founded British sporting art from Mortlake. Jacob Gole (1660-1737; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the print hop: he worked with Dusart, became an early practitioner of mezzotint, published Dusart's own mezzotints (the Communia Gaudia series including The Patriotic Peasant, c. 1695) in Amsterdam, and engraved the portrait of Adriaen van Ostade after Dusart - so he is the man who carried both Dusart and Ostade into print. His series of the Five Senses, the Seven Virtues, the Greek and Roman goddesses and the Seven Continents give a study a printmaking mechanism set (mezzotint tone, the publisher's series) that no painter in the chain so far has offered. The doorway is the engraver who published Dusart's mezzotints and engraved Ostade after him. Spares (verified, NOT part of the 7 below): Egbert van Heemskerck the Elder (1634 - c. 1702/04; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Haarlem genre painter who took the Ostade-Brouwer low life to London and turned it into satire: taverns, peasant cottages, surgical theatres, backgammon players, Quaker meetings, under the patronage of John Wilmot, 2nd Earl of Rochester. Thought to have trained with Jan Miense Molenaer (already carried as a #535 main), travelled to Italy 1655-1661, and is constantly confused with his son of the same name. His is the study of the peasant tavern become English satire. The doorway is the Haarlem satirist who took the tavern to London and Rochester. Evert Collier (26 January 1642 - few days before 8 September 1708; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the vanitas and trompe-l'oeil painter of the Haarlem guild register: probably pupil of Vincent van der Vinne, guild 1664 (the same impossible-1646 register page as Oudendijck and Heemskerck), then Leiden, Amsterdam and London from 1693. His letter racks, skulls, pocket watches, globes and hourglasses are the still-life answer to Dusart's peasant: the same Haarlem, the opposite subject, and the same London emigration as Wyck and Heemskerck. The doorway is the letter-rack painter who shared Oudendijck's impossible guild date. Adriaen Oudendijck (1677, Haarlem - 1704, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the copycat son: second son of Evert, he copied his cattle after Adriaen van de Velde and his figures after Thomas Wyck, earning the nickname "Rapianus" from Houbraken; worked with Dirk Maas, possibly in the same studio, 1695-1700; guild 1700; died in 1704, the same year as Dusart. He is the study of second-handedness at one remove from Dusart's own copying of Ostade - what it means to build a career out of other men's cattle and figures. The doorway is the copycat who died the same year as Dusart. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#535. Jacob Jordaens (19 May 1593 - 18 October 1678; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Flemish giant behind van der Cooghen: pupil of Adam van Noort (his only teacher, whose daughter he married), never went to Italy, the third of the Antwerp trio after Rubens and Van Dyck, painter of history, genre and tapestry cartoons, collaborator on the Oranjezaal at Huis ten Bosch where van der Cooghen may have trained with him. He is the biggest name in the spares and the farthest step sideways; he is a spare, not a main, because the hop runs through a single pupil (van der Cooghen) and his scale would swamp the Haarlem chain. The doorway is the Antwerp master who taught the amateur in Dusart's inventory. Rachel Ruysch (3 June 1664 - 12 October 1750; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the flower painter of the network: apprenticed at 15 to Willem van Aelst until his death in 1683, married the portraitist Juriaen Pool in 1693, ten children, court painter to the Elector Palatine Johann Wilhelm, still painting at 83. Her link to Dusart runs through Michiel van Musscher (already carried as a #535 alternate), who painted her 1692 portrait in collaboration with her - she painted the flowers, he painted her at work in her studio. She is the second woman in the chain after Judith Leyster, and the first whose career outlasted the whole Haarlem generation by half a century. The doorway is the flower painter Musscher painted at work in her own studio. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#535. Jan van Gool (1685-1763; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the biographer who wrote Dusart's life: pupil of Simon van der Does and Mattheus Terwesten, Hague Drawing School director 1720-1734, author of the Nieuwe Schouburg (1750) as an update to Houbraken, with Dusart on p. 457 and the note that his folios were "appreciated by art collectors who conserved them very carefully". A study of van Gool would be a study of how Dusart was remembered, parallel to the Houbraken spare in #535 - and of the Hague Italianate landscape he painted himself. He is the landscape answer to Dusart's cottage interior, one generation up. The doorway is the Haarlem allegorist who may have taught the young Paulus Potter. John Wootton (c. 1682 - 13 November 1764; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the English end of the Wyck line: apprenticed to Jan Wyck in the late 1690s at Mortlake, then the first English painter of horse portraits and the founder of English sporting painting at Newmarket, with patrons from Queen Anne to the Duke of Marlborough and Sir Robert Walpole. His hunting scenes carry Wyck's influence more than his horse portraits do, and he influenced George Stubbs and Thomas Gainsborough. He is the study of a Dutch import become an English institution. (The 15 names carried in #535 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Frans Hals (c. 1582 - 26 August 1666), Jan Miense Molenaer (1610 - buried 19 September 1668), Judith Leyster (bapt. 28 July 1609 - 10 February 1660) (mains); Dirck Hals (19 March 1591 - 17 May 1656) (reserve); Richard Brakenburg (bapt. 22 May 1650 - 28 December 1702), Michiel van Musscher (bapt. 27 January 1645 - 20 June 1705) (alternates); spares: Joos van Craesbeeck (c. 1605/06 - c. 1660), Hendrik Mommers (bapt. 2 January 1620 - buried 21 December 1693), Jacob Duck (c. 1600 - buried 28 January 1667), Cornelis Gerritsz Decker (1618-1678), Frans Pietersz de Grebber (1573-1649), Johannes Cornelisz Verspronck (1597-1662), Jan de Bray (d. 1697), Arnold Houbraken (28 March 1660 - 14 October 1719). Frans Hals (c. 1582 - 26 August 1666; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Ostade's teacher and the root of the whole Haarlem line: the loose, visible brushstroke, the fleeting expression, the merry company, the tronie. He taught or shaped Ostade, Brouwer, Molenaer, Leyster, Verspronck, Roestraten and van der Vinne, and his five painter sons carried the name into the next generation. His late monochrome regent pieces and his 19th-century rehabilitation (Thore-Burger, the Impressionists, van Gogh's praise) give a study a second arc beyond the teaching link. He is main 2 rather than top pick because the chain has just arrived at Ostade from below (the Boths, Bloemaert) and Dusart continues it forward, while Hals is a step back up the tree. The doorway is the teacher whose loose brush Ostade learned from and then left behind. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions of the exact phrase "Frans Hals"; no local file; not carried in #520-#534. Jan Miense Molenaer (1610 - buried 19 September 1668; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the fellow pupil who shared Hals's studio with Ostade and then shared a studio and a marriage with Judith Leyster, and the hinge between Hals and Jan Steen: his style is called a precursor to Steen's, his early work is close to Hals and his later work close to Ostade, and his Five Senses series of 1637 is the most overt visual humour of the Dutch Golden Age. He also brings the literary strand (proverbs and poems by Jacob Cats and Bredero) that Ostade never used. The doorway is the fellow pupil who married the other fellow pupil and became the forerunner of Jan Steen. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#534. FLAG: the Hals article's influence list gives his birth as 1609; his own article gives 1610. Judith Leyster (bapt. 28 July 1609 - 10 February 1660; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the woman of the Hals circle: possibly taught by Frans Pietersz de Grebber, possibly a pupil of Hals, a Guild member at 24 (1633), running her own workshop with pupils, suing Hals himself over a stolen apprentice, then marrying Molenaer in 1636. Her entire oeuvre - only eighteen works attributed with certainty, nearly all from 1628-1635 - was filed under Hals's or her husband's name for close to 200 years until Hofstede de Groot's rediscovery in 1893, when a "Frans Hals" in the Louvre turned out to be signed with her JL-star monogram. She is the strongest non-pupil hop from Ostade and the only woman in the immediate network. The doorway is the woman of the Hals circle whose whole oeuvre was filed under her husband's name for two centuries. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#534. Reserve: Dirck Hals (19 March 1591 - 17 May 1656; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Frans's younger brother and the inventor, with Willem Buytewech and Esaias van de Velde, of the merry-company picture: small, colourful, fashionable young people at music, cards and conversation, painted from about 1618-1620 to the end of his life, with a late Leiden period (1641-1648) and a collaboration line with Dirck van Delen. He influenced Nathaniel Bacon, Johann Hulsman and Willem Cornelisz Duyster, and his son Anthonie carried the name on. He is the reserve because his mechanism set (the polite merry company) is the mirror image of Ostade's peasant tavern, which makes him a strong contrast study whenever the chain wants one. The doorway is the younger brother who turned the merry company into a genre of its own. Alternates if any of the 4 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Richard Brakenburg (bapt. 22 May 1650 - 28 December 1702; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the pupil who imitated the wrong master: taught by Ostade (and Hendrik Mommers) but, per Hofstede de Groot, he imitated Jan Steen more closely than either teacher, in style, character types and the arrangement of weddings, merrymakings and quack-doctors, with figures repeated over and over and little invention of his own. His pupil Hendrik de Valk had less still. He is the study of second-handedness itself: what happens when a painter copies the copyist, and where repetition stops being a style. The doorway is the pupil who copied Steen more faithfully than his own master. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#534. Michiel van Musscher (bapt. 27 January 1645 - 20 June 1705; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the pupil with the most documented education in the network: he kept his own record - two months with Martinus Saeghmolen (1660), Abraham van den Tempel (1661), seven lessons with Gabriel Metsu (1665), three months with Ostade in Haarlem (1667) - then became Amsterdam's sought-after portraitist, painting Tsar Peter the Great and the Russian Great Embassy of 1697, and teaching Ottmar Elliger the Younger and Dirk Valkenburg. He is the study of the pupil who outgrew the peasant genre entirely and took Ostade's characterisation into high portraiture. The doorway is the pupil who took seven lessons from Metsu, three months from Ostade, and became Amsterdam's portraitist. His pupils Adriaen Rombouts and Lucas Viters extend the chain one more step. The doorway is the baker of the Antwerp citadel who learned to paint from a prisoner. The doorway is the Haarlem market painter who taught Dirk Maas and travelled to Italy with Berchem. Jacob Duck (c. 1600 - buried 28 January 1667; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the goldsmith turned painter: master goldsmith in Utrecht in 1619, drawing lessons from Droochsloot from 1621, active in Haarlem 1636-1646 alongside Ostade, and the central painter of the guardroom (kortegaardje) and the brothel scene, blending Utrecht Caravaggist humour with Haarlem merry-company painting. His is the study of the soldier's tavern where Ostade painted the peasant's. The doorway is the goldsmith turned guardroom painter who learned drawing from Droochsloot. He is the landscape answer to Ostade's cottage interior. The doorway is the Haarlem landscapist who painted in the manner of Ruisdael and shared Ostade's streets. He is the control case for Hals. The doorway is the neat portraitist against whom Hals's loose brush is measured. Jan Steen (1626-1679; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Leiden genre painter; drew technical knowledge from Jan van Goyen, Nicolaus Knupfer and Adriaen van Ostade (Ostade's pupil, per the Ostade page read end to end by leg 3). The great comic moralist of Dutch peasant life - the tavern, the household in disorder - a direct downstream heir of the Brouwer/Ostade low-life line Andries belongs to. Paulus Potter (bapt. 20 November 1625 - bur. 17 January 1654; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - animal and landscape painter; The Bull (1647, Mauritshuis), painted at 22, made the animal piece a heroic genre; about 100 paintings in a nine-year career, dead of tuberculosis at 28 - the same brief, bright arc as Andries (dead at 29/30). The landscape-with-livestock half of the Both brothers' world, from the Dutch side. The doorway is the bull painter who died at twenty-eight after making the animal heroic. The counterweight to Jan Both's Italianate landscape inside the same generation. The doorway is the native light after the Roman gold. The Flemish, courtly, prolific counterpart to Ostade - a strong study if the Haarlem line is deferred. The doorway is the courtly Fleming who painted the peasant for the Archduke. Pieter van Laer (1599 - c. 1642; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - called Il Bamboccio, the Rome contemporary Hendrick almost certainly brushed against: settled in Rome on the Via Margutta in 1625, the year before Hendrick was registered there, and turned Caravaggesque chiaroscuro away from saints and card-players and onto beggars, peddlers and mule-drivers in the Campagna - the bambocciate, taken up by Andries Both, Jan Both and Jan Asselijn. His bent-name in the Schildersbent was "de Snuffelaar". The doorway is the man who took the candlelight down to the street. The doorway is the successor who ran the same guild a generation later. (The 13 names carried in #531 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Cornelis Bloemaert II (1603 - 28 September 1692; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - second son, painter turned engraver; studied with his father, his brothers Hendrick and Adriaen, and Gerard van Honthorst; learned printmaking from Crispijn van de Passe the Elder. Paris 1630 (engravings for Michel de Marolles's Temple des Muses), Rome from 1633 until his death - 59 years, most of his career. Bentvueghels bent-name "Winter". His Roman workshop attracted young engravers from abroad and spread Italian art through Europe; best-known engravings after Annibale Carracci (The Holy Family), Pietro da Cortona (Adoration of the Shepherds) and Rubens (Meleager); 1664-77 he worked on the engraved record of Cortona's Palazzo Pitti frescoes. Pupils Michel Natalis and Gilles Rousselet. He delayed returning to see his dying father so long that his father died - he stayed in Rome. The doorway is the son who went: the father never went to Italy and learned Caravaggism second-hand - now follow the son who did go, stayed 59 years, and engraved Italy for the rest of Europe. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Death date flagged as disputed: Wikipedia gives 28 September 1692; the NGA gives "in or after 1684" and Te Papa "c. 1684"; an older cyclopedia says 1680. 1692 is the commonly cited date. Leonaert Bramer (24 December 1596 - buried 10 February 1674; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Delft painter of genre, religious and history scenes, listed by the RKD among Bloemaert's pupils. At 18 he left on a long trip reaching Rome in 1616 via Arras, Amiens, Paris, Aix, Marseille, Genoa and Livorno; in Rome he was a founder of the Bentvueghels (bent-name "Nestelg...") and lived with Wouter Crabeth, once got into a fight with Claude Lorrain, and acquired the sobriquet "Leonardo della Notte" - Leonardo of the Night - for his nocturnes. He met Wybrand de Geest in Aix-en-Provence in 1616. Back in Delft by 1628, Guild of St. Luke 1629; painted frescoes, a rarity north of the Alps (not survived), and murals on canvas. A lifelong bachelor, productive until his death at 77; it is possible, though undocumented, that Vermeer trained with him. One of the most intriguing personalities in 17th-century Dutch art. The doorway is the Delft pupil who ran off to Rome at eighteen, helped found the Bentvueghels, fought with Claude Lorrain, and came home to paint the night. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Jan Baptist Weenix (1621-1659; Leiden Collection gives 1660/61 - death date disputed, flag it; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - painter of the Dutch Golden Age, Bloemaert pupil (after Jan Micker, before Claes Moeyaert). Born near Amsterdam harbour, son of an architect; travelled to Italy 1643-47, joined the Bentvueghels as "Ratel" (rattle, for a speech defect), worked for Cardinal Camillo Pamphili (later Pope Innocent X), painted a portrait of Rene Descartes, and in 1649 became master of the Utrecht Guild of St. Luke alongside Jan Both as officer. Mainly responsible for introducing the Italian harbour scene into Dutch art - mid-size paintings with a group of figures in the foreground - alongside Italian landscapes with large figures among ruins, seaside views, and late still lifes of dead game and dogs. With Berchem and Jan Both, a leader of the second generation of Dutch Italianate painters; teacher of his son Jan Weenix and nephew Melchior d'Hondecoeter. Died at 39 at Huis ter Mey, Vleuten. The doorway is the Utrecht pupil who took the master's Italianate landscape and turned it into the Dutch harbour scene - and died at thirty-nine, leaving a son who became the great still-life painter. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. (Mains 2, 3 and 4; main 1 is the top pick above.) Reserve: Wybrand Simonsz. de Geest (16 August 1592 - c. 1661; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Frisian portrait painter, the most important portraitist of Friesland. Learned first from his father Simon Juckesz, a stained-glass worker, then studied with Bloemaert in Utrecht. Grand Tour 1614-18 through France and Italy; met Leonaert Bramer in Aix-en-Provence in 1616; in Rome joined the Bentvueghels as "De Friesche Adelaar" (the Frisian Eagle) and kept an album amicorum (friendship book) from 1611, 22 drawings from which are still missing. Returned to Leeuwarden c. 1620 and became the leading portrait painter of the Frisian nobility and the Orange-Nassau family; married Hendrickje Fransdr Uylenburgh in 1622, niece of Saskia van Uylenburgh - Rembrandt's wife - linking him by marriage to Rembrandt's circle; influenced Jacob Adriaensz Backer; students Jan Jansz. de Stomme and Jacob Potma; sons Julius and Frank also became painters. The doorway is the Frisian pupil who carried Utrecht portraiture north to Leeuwarden, married into Rembrandt's family, and signed his Roman years "the Frisian Eagle". VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Alternates if any of the 5 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Jacob Gerritsz. Cuyp (1594-1651/52; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dordrecht portrait and landscape painter, RKD-listed pupil of Bloemaert; son of stained-glass designer Gerrit Gerritsz. Cuyp, half-brother of Benjamin Gerritsz. Cuyp, father of the far more famous Aelbert Cuyp. Joined the Dordrecht Guild of St. Luke 18 July 1617, treasurer 1629/33/37/41; helped found the Dordrecht painters' breakaway guild in 1642 and is called the nestor of the Dordrecht painters. Best known today for portraits - especially fine portraits of children - and pastoral landscapes; early work shows distinct Caravaggesque influence from his Utrecht training. Teacher of Ferdinand Bol, Aelbert Cuyp, Benjamin Cuyp, Paulus Lesire, Aert van der Neer and Pieter Hermansz Verelst; Jacob's recommendation is said to have sent Nicolaes Maes and Ferdinand Bol to Rembrandt. The doorway is the Utrecht pupil who went home to Dordrecht, founded its painting school, and taught the father of the golden light - Aelbert Cuyp's father, and Rembrandt's Bol. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Birth year flagged: 1594 is the current consensus against 1575 in the 9th edition of the Britannica - the old date is wrong, use 1594. Nicolaus Knupfer (1609 - 15 October 1655; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch Golden Age painter, born Leipzig, apprenticed there to Emanuel Nysen, then Magdeburg making brushes for artists until 1630, when he moved to Utrecht to work with Bloemaert - living with him for two years - before setting up his own Utrecht studio (visiting member of the Guild of St. Luke, 1637). Painted small-scale biblical, literary and mythological scenes and popular genre works; worked on the decorations of Kronborg Castle in Denmark; painted the figures in the landscapes of Jan Both and Jan Baptist Weenix - the staffage hand inside two other Bloemaert pupils' pictures. A successful teacher: his students included Jan Steen, Gabriel Metsu, Ary de Vois and Pieter Crijnse Volmarijn. The doorway is the Leipzig brush-maker who lived two years in the master's house, then painted the little figures inside Jan Both's and Weenix's landscapes - and taught Jan Steen. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Spares (verified, NOT part of the 7): Hieronymus Francken I (c. 1540-1610; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Flemish painter, pupil of Frans Floris, worked at Fontainebleau 1566-72, court painter to the French court from 1594; Bloemaert's Paris/Fontainebleau master 1581-83. Elegant Mannerist groups of dancers, musicians and courtiers, uniting Antwerp, Venetian and French elements. Brother of Frans Francken I and Ambrosius Francken I; father of a painting dynasty. The doorway is the Fontainebleau master who gave the boy from Gorinchem the only real training he ever got. Dates as commonly cited (c. 1614/17) - flag as approximate. THIN RECORD WARNING: known chiefly as his father's publisher/engraver rather than for independent work. Andries Both (1612/13 - 23 March 1642; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - elder brother of Jan Both, Bloemaert pupil; Rouen 1633, Rome from 1635, shared a studio with Jan van Causteren, then lived with his brother on the Via Vittoria from 1638; a leading bambocciante - low-life Roman genre scenes after Pieter van Laer, influenced by Brueghel and Adriaen Brouwer - while his brother painted idyllic landscapes. Drowned in a Venetian canal in 1642, aged 29 or 30, on the way home. The doorway is the elder Both brother, who painted Rome's low life while his brother painted its golden light - and drowned on the way home.
